In this paper, we develop an alternative, genuinely quantum approach to error reduction, that we call a
purifier
.
The main feature is that, unlike majority voting, the query complexity of a purifier stays bounded by a constant
no matter how small the error
\varepsilon
is
.
This is effectively equivalent to having an errorless algorithm (although, we cannot obtain an exact algorithm with a finite overhead in general).
We prove the following theorem in Section
13
.

Theorem 3.14

(Purifier)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a canonical transducer
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
with bidirectional access to an oracle like in (
3.32
) that has the following properties.
Assuming the oracle satisfies (
3.31
) or (
3.33
), the purifier transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
into
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(\psi)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
with error at most
\varepsilon
(in the sense to be made exact in Section
12
).
Both its transduction and query complexities are bounded by a constant.
Its time complexity is
\mathcal{O}\bigl(s\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigr)
in the circuit model, and
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
in the QRAG model, where
s
is the number of qubits used by
\mathcal{M}
.

The purifier is inspired by the corresponding construction in
[
14
]
, which used the dual adversary bound.
It had the same characteristic property of query complexity being bounded by a constant, but there are some differences.

•

The purifiers in
[
14
]
worked solely in the
query
complexity settings.
The resulting dual adversary bound was for
exact
function evaluation.
Also, by the nature of the adversary bound, it was implicitly assumed that there is only a
finite
collection of possible input oracles, and, technically, for different collections, we obtain different purifiers.

•

The purifiers in the current paper are constructed keeping both query and
time
complexity in mind.
Also, the same purifier works for all (
infinitely many
) possible input oracles.

•

Due to these improvements, the purifier ceases to be exact, but introduces a small error.

Proof sketch of Theorem
3.14
.

We consider the Boolean case, as the general case can be easily obtained using the Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm
[
18
]
as in, e.g.,
[
29
, Section 4]
.
Our construction is a quantum walk in the sense of Section
3.2
.
In particular, it transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
into
(-1)^{f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.

The overall structure is given by the following toy transducer
S_{\mathrm{toy}}
in Figure
3.6
.
It is a quantum walk on the one-sided infinite line.
That is,
S_{\mathrm{toy}}=R_{2}R_{1}
, where the reflection
R_{1}
is the product of the local reflections about the odd vertices
1,3,5,\cdots
, and
R_{2}
about the positive even vertices
2,4,6,\cdots
.
The local reflection at the vertex
i>0
is given by the
X
operation, which maps

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

(3.34)

(a)
0
1
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
2
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
3
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
4
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|3}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
5
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|4}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
\cdots
(b)
0
1
1.0
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
2
-1.0
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
3
1.0
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
4
-1.0
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|3}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
5
1.0
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|4}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
\cdots

Now, it is not hard to see that in the negative and the positive case, we have the following mappings, which follow the general case of (
3.4
) and (
3.5
):

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{\mathrm{toy}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}(-1)^{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{\mathrm{toy}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}(-1)^{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

(3.35)

This formally gives us both
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{\mathrm{toy}}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{\mathrm{toy}}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
Of course, this does not contradict Theorem
3.1
, because the corresponding space has infinite dimension and both catalysts in (
3.35
) have infinite norm.

Nonetheless, we will be able to utilise this general construction.
The first order of business is to reduce the norm of the catalysts.
Our next step towards a purifier will be a multidimensional quantum walk on the line, in the sense that each edge corresponds to a multidimensional subspace.
See Figure
3.6
.

(a)
0
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
2
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
3
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
4
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|3}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
5
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|4}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
1
\widetilde{\psi}
\widetilde{\psi}^{\otimes 2}
\widetilde{\psi}^{\otimes 3}
\widetilde{\psi}^{\otimes 4}
\cdots
(b)
0
1
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
2
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
3
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
4
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|3}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
5
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|4}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
1
-\widetilde{\psi}
\widetilde{\psi}^{\otimes 2}
-\widetilde{\psi}^{\otimes 3}
\widetilde{\psi}^{\otimes 4}
\cdots

We will define a vector
\widetilde{\psi}
that depends on
\psi
and satisfies
\|\widetilde{\psi}\|=1-\Omega(1)
.
The initial coupling is given by

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{i\cdot f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i},

and the transduction complexity is bounded by

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\|\widetilde{\psi}\|^{2i}=\mathcal{O}(1).

Let us now explain how to implement the local reflections (
3.34
) for this modified transducer.
Up to a sign, the content of the space incident to a vertex
i>0
is given by

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i-1}+(-1)^{f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i}.

(3.36)

We use the input oracle to obtain the state

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+(-1)^{f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i}.

(3.37)

Later we can get back from (
3.37
) to (
3.36
) by uncomputing the
\psi
.
Therefore, bringing the term
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i-1}
outside the brackets, it suffices to implement the mapping

\begin{cases}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle,&\text{if $f(\psi)=0$;\; and}\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle\mapsto-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle,&\text{if $f(\psi)=1$.}\end{cases}

(3.38)

This is where the condition (
3.31
) comes into play.
We use the same rescaling idea as in
[
14
]
, and define the state

\widetilde{\psi}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{a}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},&\text{if $f(\psi)=0$};\\
a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},&\text{if $f(\psi)=1$}.\\
\end{cases}

with

a=\sqrt[4]{\frac{1-c+d}{1-c-d}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad b=\sqrt[4]{\frac{c+d}{c-d}}.

It is not hard to check that
\|\widetilde{\psi}\|^{2}=1-\Omega(1)
.
Now the operation in (
3.38
) reads as

\begin{cases}\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{a}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{a}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},\\
\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}-a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}-\frac{1}{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\bigl(-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\bigl(-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},\end{cases}

which can be implemented as the 2-qubit reflection about the span of the states

\bigl(a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

Following the same logic as in the toy example, we obtain that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}(-1)^{f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
However, this time, both the transduction and the query complexities are bounded by a constant.
The problem is that this construction still requires infinite space.
We solve this by truncating the line after some vertex
D
.
This introduces an error, but since the norms of the vectors decrease exponentially with
i
, it suffices to take
D=\mathcal{O}\bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigr)
.
Finally, the transducer can be converted into a canonical form by the standard technique of Proposition
10.4
.
∎

The main purpose of purifiers is to reduce error.
Let us give few examples.
First, recall the definition of the composed function
f\circ g
from (
3.29
):

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}f\circ g\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}

\displaystyle(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m},\;\;z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m},\;\;\dots\dots,\;\;z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})

\displaystyle=f\bigl(g(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m}),g(z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m}),\dots,g(z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})\bigr).

We use notation

\vec{y}_{i}=(z_{i,1},\dots,z_{i,m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad x=\bigl(g(\vec{y}_{1}),g(\vec{y}_{2}),\dots g(\vec{y}_{n})\bigr)

so that
f(x)=\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}f\circ g\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}(z)
.
In the circuit model, we have the following result on the evaluation of this function.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
A
and
B
be quantum algorithms in the circuit model that evaluate the functions
f
and
g
, respectively, with bounded error.
Then, there exists a quantum algorithm in the circuit model that evaluates the function
f\circ g
with bounded error in time complexity

\mathcal{O}(L)\bigl(T(A)+T(B)+s\log L\bigr)

(3.39)

where
L
is the worst-case Las Vegas query complexity of
A
, and
s
is the space complexity of
B
.

Proof sketch.

We obtain the transducer
S_{A}
in the circuit model using Theorem
3.5
.
The transduction and query complexities of
S_{A}
are bounded by
L
.
Let
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
be the purifier for the input oracle
B
.
Consider the transducer
S=S_{A}\circ\bigoplus_{i}S_{\mathrm{pur}}
, see Figure
3.6
.
The transducer
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
on the input oracle
B(O_{\vec{y}_{i}})
evaluates
g(y_{i})
with diminished error.
It suffices to make error somewhat smaller than
1/L
.
Then,
S
on the input oracle
\bigoplus_{i}B(O_{\vec{y}_{i}})
evaluates
f\circ g
with bounded error.

S_{A}
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
B
O_{\vec{y}_{1}}
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
B
O_{\vec{y}_{2}}
\cdots
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
B
O_{\vec{y}_{n}}

The transduction complexity of
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
on a unit admissible vector is
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Hence, by (
3.17
)

W(S,O_{z},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})\leq W(S_{A},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})+\mathcal{O}(1)\cdot L(S_{A},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=\mathcal{O}(L).

All the purifiers can be implemented in parallel, hence by Proposition
3.7
,

T(S)=T(S_{A})+T(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(A)\bigr)+\mathcal{O}(s\log L).

The theorem follows from Theorem
3.2
applied to
S
, where we replace execution of the input oracle by the execution of
B
.
Again, all the
B
can be executed in parallel.
∎

In the QRAG model, we can easily deal with different functions
g_{i}
, like in the following function, which we already considered in (
2.1
):

f\bigl(g_{1}(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m}),g_{2}(z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m}),\dots,g_{n}(z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})\bigr).

(3.40)

We again use notation

\vec{y}_{i}=(z_{i,1},\dots,z_{i,m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad x=\bigl(g_{1}(\vec{y}_{1}),g_{2}(\vec{y}_{2}),\dots g_{n}(\vec{y}_{n})\bigr).

Theorem 3.16
.

Consider the function as in (
3.40
).
Let
A
and
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
be quantum algorithms that evaluate the functions
f
and
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
, respectively, with bounded error.
Assuming the QRAG model and QRAM access to the description of
A
and
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
, there exists a quantum algorithm that evaluates the function (
3.40
) with bounded error in time complexity

\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})\max_{x}\Bigl(T(A)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}T(B_{i})L^{(i)}_{x}(A)\Bigr).

(3.41)

Here
L^{(i)}_{x}(A)
is the
i
-th partial Las Vegas complexity
L^{(i)}\bigl(A,O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)
of the algorithm
A
on the input oracle encoding
x
.

Proof sketch.

We obtain the transducer
S_{A}
in the QRAG model using Theorem
3.5
.
Its transduction complexity is
T(A)
.
We assume all
B_{i}
have the same range and the same error.
Let
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
be the corresponding purifier.
Finally, let
S_{B_{i}}
be the transducer in the QRAG model corresponding to
B_{i}
.
Consider the composed transducer
S
as in Figure
3.6
.
Similarly to Theorem
3.15
,
the transducer
S
on the input oracle
O_{z}=\bigoplus_{i}O_{\vec{y}_{i}}
evaluates the function
f\circ g
with bounded error provided that the error of the purifier is sufficiently smaller than
1/L
.

S_{A}
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
S_{B_{1}}
O_{\vec{y}_{1}}
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
S_{B_{2}}
O_{\vec{y}_{2}}
\cdots
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
S_{B_{n}}
O_{\vec{y}_{n}}

Using that the transduction and the query complexity of
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
are
\mathcal{O}(1)
, we obtain that

W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}}\circ S_{B_{i}},O_{\vec{y}_{i}})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(B_{i})\bigr).

Therefore, by (
3.21
) and using that
A
and
S_{A}
have the same query state:

W\bigl(S,O_{z},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\leq W(S_{A},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})+\sum_{i}\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(B_{i})\bigr)L^{(i)}\bigl(A,O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr).

For the time complexity,
S
is a composition of three transducers, where the last two are direct sums. All three of them have time complexity
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
, hence, this is also the time complexity of
S
.
The theorem follows from Theorem
3.2
.
∎

Comparison between Theorems
3.15
and
3.16
is similar to the comparison between Theorems
3.8
and
3.10
in Section
3.5
.
The second theorem considers a more general case (
3.40
), and its formulation is close to (
2.4
).
On the other hand, in (
3.39
),
T(B)
will most likely dominate
s\log L
, so the latter can be removed.
Also, if
T(A)
is smaller than
T(B)
, the whole expression is dominated by
L\cdot T(B)
, which is what we would like to have.

Finally, in the QRAG model, any bounded-error quantum algorithm
A
can be turned into an essentially exact transducer
S_{A}
such that, up to constant factors, its transduction complexity is
T(A)
and its query complexity is the query complexity of
A
.
The latter transducers can be composed in multiple layers as in Theorem
3.11
.

4
Preliminaries

If not said otherwise, a
vector space
is a finite-dimensional complex inner product space. They are denoted by calligraphic letters. We assume that each vector space has a fixed orthonormal basis, and we often identify an operator with the corresponding matrix.

I_{\mathcal{X}}
stands for the identity operator in
\mathcal{X}
.
The inner product is denoted by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot}}\right\rangle
.

A^{*}
stands for the adjoint linear operator.
All projectors are orthogonal projectors.
We use ket-notation to emphasise that a vector is a state of a quantum register, or to denote the elements of the computational basis.

We use
\mathcal{O}
for the big-Oh notation in order to distinguish from
O
, which we use for input oracles.

\mathcal{O}_{A}
means that the constant may depend on
A
.
If
P
is a predicate, we use
1_{P}
to denote the corresponding indicator variable; which is equal to 1 if
P
is true, and to 0 otherwise.

4.1
Query Algorithms

In this section, we briefly describe the model of quantum query algorithms, and define quantum Las Vegas query complexity.
The query model itself is essentially standard
[
20
,
22
]
, with the main difference that we do not restrict ourselves to the evaluation of functions, and also allow for multiple input oracles, which can be arbitrary unitaries.
The notion of quantum Las Vegas query complexity is relatively new
[
17
]
.

A quantum query algorithm
A
works in space
\mathcal{H}
, which we call the
workspace
of the algorithm.
The algorithm is given an oracle
O
, which is a unitary
10
10

10

While
[
17
]
define more general input oracles, we, for simplicity, consider only unitary input oracles in this paper.

in some space
\mathcal{M}
.
The interaction between the algorithm and the oracle is in the form of queries, which we are about to define.
The workspace is decomposed as
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
, where the oracle is only applied to the second half.
Also,
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{H}^{\uparrow}\otimes\mathcal{M}
for some
\mathcal{H}^{\uparrow}
, and the
query
is

\widetilde{O}=I^{\circ}\oplus I\otimes O,

(4.1)

where
I^{\circ}
and
I
are the identities in
\mathcal{H}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\uparrow}
, respectively.

In terms of registers, we assume the decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
is marked by a register
\mathcal{R}
so that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
corresponds to
\mathcal{H}^{\circ}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
.
Then, the query
\widetilde{O}
is an application of
O
, controlled by
\mathcal{R}
, where
O
acts on some subset of the registers (which constitute
\mathcal{M}
).

The
quantum query algorithm

A=A(O)
is a sequence of linear transformations in
\mathcal{H}
:

A(O)=U_{Q}\,\widetilde{O}\,U_{Q-1}\,\widetilde{O}\,\cdots U_{1}\,\widetilde{O}\,U_{0},

(4.2)

where
U_{t}
are some input-independent unitaries in
\mathcal{H}
.
See Figure
4.1
.
Thus, the algorithm implements a transformation
O\mapsto A(O)
: from the input oracle
O
in
\mathcal{M}
to the linear operator (
4.2
) in
\mathcal{H}
.

\xi
U_{0}
I\otimes O
U_{1}
\psi_{1}^{\circ}
\psi_{1}^{\bullet}
I\otimes O
U_{2}
\psi_{2}^{\circ}
\psi_{2}^{\bullet}
I\otimes O
U_{3}
\psi_{3}^{\circ}
\psi_{3}^{\bullet}
\tau

We will generally work with the state conversion formalism. We say that
A
transforms
\xi
into
\tau
on oracle
O
, if
A(O)\xi=\tau
.
We say that
A
does so
\varepsilon
-approximately if
\bigl\lVert\tau-A(O)\xi\bigr\rVert\leq\varepsilon
.
In this context, we will often call
\varepsilon
the
error
of the algorithm.

Let us make two important remarks on the structure of thus defined query algorithms.

Remark 4.1

(Alignment)
.

Note that all the queries in (
4.2
) are identical, i.e., the oracle
O
is always applied to the same registers and is always controlled by
\mathcal{R}
.
To acknowledge this, we say that all the queries in
A
are
aligned
.
Usually, this is not important, but the alignment property will play a significant role in this paper, in particular in Sections
9
and
10
.
The main reason is that for the aligned program we can perform all the queries in parallel (assuming we have the intermediate states
\psi_{t}
from Figure
4.1
somehow).

Remark 4.2

(Unidirectionality)
.

The input oracle is unidirectional: the algorithm only has access to
O
.
This suffices for most of our results.
Quite often, however, bidirectional access to the input oracle is allowed, where the algorithm can query both
O
and
O^{*}
.
The latter is a special case of the former, as bidirectional access to
O
is equivalent to unidirectional access to
O\oplus O^{*}
.
As this situation will be common in some sections of the paper, we utilise the following piece of notation:

\overleftrightarrow{O}=O\oplus O^{*}.

(4.3)

The standard complexity measure of a quantum query algorithm is
Q=Q(A)
: the total number of times the query
\widetilde{O}
is executed.
It was called Monte Carlo complexity in
[
17
]
in order to distinguish it from Las Vegas complexity defined next.

Let
\Pi^{\bullet}
be the projector onto
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
.
The state processed by the oracle on the
t
-th query is
\psi_{t}^{\bullet}=\Pi^{\bullet}U_{t-1}\,\widetilde{O}\,U_{t-2}\cdots\widetilde{O}\,U_{0}
, and the
total query state
is

q(A,O,\xi)=\bigoplus_{t=1}^{Q}\psi_{t}^{\bullet}.

(4.4)

This is the most complete way of specifying the work performed by the input oracle
O
in the algorithm
A
on the initial state
\xi
.
It is a member of
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{M}
for some space
\mathcal{E}
(the latter actually being equal to
\mathbb{C}^{Q}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\uparrow}
).
The simplest way to gauge the total query state is by defining the corresponding
quantum Las Vegas query complexity
:

L(A,O,\xi)=\|q(A,O,\xi)\|^{2}.

(4.5)

As mentioned in Section
2.3
, we extend the definitions (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) for the case
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{H}
for some
\mathcal{E}
using identities (
2.10
) and (
2.11
).

4.2
Multiple Input Oracles

It is also possible for an algorithm to have access to several input oracles
O^{(1)},\dots,O^{(r)}
.
Las Vegas query complexity can naturally accommodate such a scenario.
Indeed, access to several input oracles is equivalent to access to the one combined oracle

O=O^{(1)}\oplus O^{(2)}\oplus\cdots\oplus O^{(r)}.

(4.6)

Consequently, the space of the oracle has a similar decomposition
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{(1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{M}^{(r)}
, where
O^{(i)}
acts in
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}
.
The total query state can also be decomposed into
partial query states

q(A,O,\xi)=q^{(1)}(A,O,\xi)\oplus q^{(2)}(A,O,\xi)\oplus\cdots\oplus q^{(r)}(A,O,\xi),

where
q^{(i)}(A,O,\xi)
is processed by
O^{(i)}
.
This gives partial Las Vegas query complexities

L^{(i)}(A,O,\xi)=\bigl\lVert q^{(i)}(A,O,\xi)\bigr\rVert^{2}.

In terms of registers, it can be assumed that the input oracle is controlled by some register
\mathcal{R}
, where the value
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
indicates no application of the input oracle, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
with
i>0
indicates the
i
-th input oracle
O^{(i)}
.
We note that we use
i
in
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
only as a label.
In particular, we do not perform any arithmetical operations on them.
Therefore,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
can have a complicated internal encoding that can facilitate the application of the oracle.

The assumption on the register
\mathcal{R}
in this section is not necessarily in contradiction with the assumptions of Section
4.1
, as
\mathcal{R}
can have a separate qubit that indicates whether
i
in
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
is non-zero.
This qubit can serve as
\mathcal{R}
in the sense of Section
4.1
.

The case of usual Monte Carlo query complexity requires additional changes, as per now it turns out that all the oracles are queried the same number of times,
Q
.
One way to allow for different number of queries is as follows.
Similarly to (
4.1
), define the query to the
i
-th input oracle as

\widetilde{O}^{(i)}=I^{\circ}\oplus I\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I^{(1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus I^{(i-1)}\oplus O^{(i)}\oplus I^{(i+1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus I^{(r)}}}\right),

where the decomposition in the brackets is the same as in (
4.6
).
In other words,
\widetilde{O}^{(i)}
is just the application of
O^{(i)}
controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.
The query algorithm is then defined as

A(O)=U_{Q}\,\widetilde{O}^{(s_{Q})}\,U_{Q-1}\,\widetilde{O}^{(s_{Q-1})}\,\cdots\,U_{2}\,\widetilde{O}^{(s_{2})}\,U_{1}\,\widetilde{O}^{(s_{1})}\,U_{0},

(4.7)

where
s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{Q}\in[r]
.
The number of invocations of the
i
-th oracle,
Q^{(i)}
, is defined as the number of
s_{j}
equal to
i
in (
4.7
).

4.3
Evaluation of Functions

The
standard
settings for quantum algorithms evaluating a (partial) function
f\colon D\to[p]
with
D\subseteq[q]^{n}
are as follows.
For an input
x\in[q]^{n}
, the corresponding input oracle acts in
\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{q}
as

O_{x}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}

(4.8)

for all
i\in[n]
and
b\in[q]
.
Here
\oplus
stands for the bitwise XOR, and we assume that
q
is a power of 2 (we can ignore the inputs outside of the domain).

The algorithm
A
itself has a special output register isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{p}
.
After finishing the algorithm, measuring the output register should yield the value
f(x)
.
This either happens with probability 1 (for exact algorithms), or with probability at least
1/2+d
for some constant
d>0
(bounded error).

This definition is nice because there is one well-defined input oracle
O_{x}
for each input.
Also
O_{x}^{2}=I
, which makes uncomputing very easy.
Unfortunately, the standard definition has a drawback that the input oracle of the algorithm has a more restricted form than the one required for the algorithm itself.
This is problematic if the algorithm is expected to be used as an input oracle for another algorithm.
This issue is solved by noting that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
can be implemented by evaluating
f(x)
, performing the XOR operation, and uncomputing
f(x)
.
This increases the complexity by a factor of 2, which is fine if we ignore constant factors.
We call it
robust
evaluation of function, as the action of the algorithm is specified for all input states, not just
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.

However, constant factors can be important, for instance, in iterated functions, where such factors appear as a base of the exponent, or in the settings of Las Vegas complexity in
[
17
]
, where precise complexity is sought for.
In this case, a more homogenous definition would be appreciated.

We follow one such approach from
[
14
]
, which we call
state-generating
.
We say that the input oracle
O_{x}
encodes the input string
x\in[q]^{n}
if it performs the transformation

O_{x}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}

(4.9)

for all
i\in[n]
.
The action of this oracle on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
is identical to that of (
4.8
), but we do not require anything for other states.
In other words, the admissible subspace of
O_{x}
consists of vectors having
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
in the second register.
The admissible subspace of the algorithm itself is spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
On that, it has to perform the transformation
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
The algorithm has bidirectional access to
O_{x}
, which we treat as unidirectional access to
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
defined in (
4.3
).
It is possible to assume the input oracle
O_{x}
is a direct sum of
n
unitaries acting in
\mathbb{C}^{q}
in order to apply the multiple input oracle settings from Section
7.2
.

As the initial state is always
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
, and
O_{x}
is essentially determined by
x
, we will write

L_{x}(A)=L\bigl(A,\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\qquad\text{and}\qquad L(A)=\max_{x\in D}L_{x}(A).

(4.10)

More precisely, we define
L_{x}(A)
as the supremum over all input oracles

O_{x}
that encode the input
x
.
We use similar notation for
L^{(i)}_{x}
and
L^{(i)}
.

This approach has a number of advantages.
First, it casts function evaluation as a special case of state conversion with state-generating input oracles
[
14
]
.
Second, the algorithm can be directly used as a part of the input oracle for another algorithm without any uncomputation.
Finally, this definition does not involve the somewhat arbitrary XOR operation and may be, thus, regarded as being more pure.
In particular, it makes sense to ask for the precise value of its quantum Las Vegas query complexity (and not just only up to constant factors).

This approach has a number of disadvantages.
First, we have to explicitly allow bidirectional access to
O_{x}
in order to allow uncomputing, as it is no longer the case that
O_{x}
is its own inverse.
More importantly, though, neither the action nor the Las Vegas complexity of the algorithm is specified for the initial states orthogonal to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
For our own algorithms, we can design them so that
O_{x}
is only executed with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
in the second register (maybe after some perturbation, see Section
4.6
).
But, if we are dealing with an arbitrary algorithm, we have no such guarantees.

4.4
Circuit Model

We assume that the space of the algorithm is embedded into a product of qubits,
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes s}
, for some
s
called the space complexity of the algorithm.
A quantum program is a product of elementary operations called gates:

A=G_{T}G_{T-1}\cdots G_{1}.

(4.11)

In the circuit model, each gate
G_{i}
is usually a 1- or a 2-qubit operation that can be applied to any qubit or a pair of qubits.
The number of elementary operations,
T
, is called the time complexity of
A
, and is denoted by
T(A)
.
We assume a universal gate set, so that every unitary can be written as a quantum program.
We do not focus too much on a particular model, as they are all essentially equivalent.

In a query algorithm like in Section
4.1
, each execution of the input oracle
\widetilde{O}
also traditionally counts as one elementary operation.
In other words, each unitary in (
4.2
) can be decomposed into elementary gates as in (
4.11
) to give a corresponding program in the circuit model.
We use
T
to denote its time complexity, and
Q
to denote its Monte Carlo query complexity.

We will often require an algorithm like in (
4.11
) to be executed conditionally, that is, controlled by the value of some external qubit.
In other words, we would like to perform an operation
A^{\mathrm{c}}
of the form

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A^{\mathrm{c}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A^{\mathrm{c}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|A\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},

where the first qubit is the control qubit.
We will denote time complexity of this procedure by
T_{\mathrm{C}}(A)
.

Since it is possible to substitute each
G_{i}
in (
4.11
) by its controlled version, we have that
T_{\mathrm{C}}(A)=\mathcal{O}(T(A))
.
But it is often possible to do better.
For instance, assume that
A
is of the form
A_{2}A_{1}^{\mathrm{c}}
, i.e., a large chunk of
A
is already conditioned.
We have that
T(A)=T(A_{2})+T_{\mathrm{C}}(A_{1})
.
On the other hand,
T_{\mathrm{C}}(A)=T_{\mathrm{C}}(A_{2})+T_{\mathrm{C}}(A_{1})+\mathcal{O}(1)
, because we can calculate the AND of the two control qubits of
A_{1}
into a fresh qubit, execute
A_{1}
controlled by this fresh qubit, and uncompute the new qubit afterwards.
In other words, the constant factor of
T_{\mathrm{C}}(A)=\mathcal{O}(T(A))
is not getting accumulated with each new conditioning, but is, in a way, paid only once.

4.5
QRAG model

The QRAG model extends the circuit model by allowing the following Quantum Random Access Gate:

\mathrm{QRAG}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{m}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},b,x_{i+1},\dots,x_{m}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},

where the first register is an
m
-qudit, and the remaining ones are quantum words (i.e., quantum registers large enough to index all the qubits in the program).
We assume the QRAG takes time
T_{\mathrm{R}}
as specified in (
3.13
).
Note that this gate would require time
\Omega(m)
to implement in the usual circuit model, as it depends on all
m+2
registers.

This should not be confused with the QRAM model, which allows oracle access to an array of classical registers
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{m}
:

\mathrm{QRAM}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},

where
\oplus
stands for the bit-wise XOR. The difference is that
x_{i}
s are being fixed during the execution of the quantum procedure (but they may be changed classically between different executions).
The QRAG model is more powerful than the QRAM model.

The main reason we need the QRAG is the following result (see, e.g.,
[
29
]
for a formal statement, although the same construction has been used elsewhere including
[
2
,
23
,
26
]
):

Theorem 4.3

(Select Operation)
.

Assume the QRAG model and that we have QRAM access to a description of a quantum program as in (
4.11
) in some space
\mathcal{H}
, where each gate
G_{i}
either comes from a fixed set of 1- or 2-qubit operations (which can be applied to different qubits each time), or is a QRAG.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
T
-qudit. Then, the following Select operation

\sum_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{I}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}\mapsto\sum_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{I}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|G_{i}\psi_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}

(4.12)

can be implemented in time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.

Proof sketch.

We add a number of scratch registers to perform the following operations.
Conditioned on
i
, we use the QRAM to read the description of
G_{i}
.
We switch the arguments of
G_{i}
into the scratch space using the QRAG.
We apply the operation
G_{i}
on the scratch space.
We switch the arguments back into memory, and erase the description of
G_{i}
from the scratch memory.
All the operations besides applying
G_{i}
take time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.
Application of a usual gate
G_{i}
takes time
\mathcal{O}(1)
as the gate set is fixed, or
\mathcal{O}(T_{R})
if
G_{i}
is a QRAG.
∎

It is also possible to not store the whole program in memory, but compute it on the fly, in which case the complexity of this computation should be added to the complexity stated in Theorem
4.3
.

Corollary 4.4

(Parallel execution of programs)
.

Assume that in the settings of Theorem
4.3
we have QRAM access to an array storing descriptions of
m
quantum programs
A^{(1)},\dots,A^{(m)}
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a
m
-qudit. Then, the following operation

\sum_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{I}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}\mapsto\sum_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{I}}\bigl|A^{(i)}\psi_{i}\bigr>_{\mathcal{H}},

can be implemented in time
\mathcal{O}\bigl(T_{\mathrm{R}}\cdot\max_{i}T(A^{(i)})\bigr)
.

Proof sketch.

Use Theorem
4.3
to implement the first gate in all of
A^{(i)}
in parallel, then the second one, and so on until the time mark
\max_{i}T(A^{(i)})
.
∎

Another important primitive is the random access (RA) input oracle.
If
O\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
is an input oracle, then its RA version acts on
\mathcal{J}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes K}
, where
\mathcal{J}
is a
K
-qudit.
If the register
\mathcal{J}
contains value
i
, the input oracle is applied to the
i
-th copy of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\otimes K}
.

The idea behind this is that if
O
is implemented as a subroutine, then the RA input oracle is a special case of Corollary
4.4
, where all
A^{(i)}
are the same, but act on different sets of registers (which is easy to define using
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}
as an offset).
Therefore, it makes sense to define the RA input oracle as an elementary operation in the QRAG model.

4.6
Perturbed Algorithms

The following lemma is extremely useful in quantum algorithms, but for some reason has seldom experienced the honour of being explicitly stated.

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume we have a collection of unitaries
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
all acting in the same vector space
\mathcal{H}
.
Let
\psi_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\psi_{m}^{\prime}
be a collection of vectors in
\mathcal{H}
such that

\psi_{t}^{\prime}=U_{t}\psi_{t-1}^{\prime}

for all
t
.
Let
\psi_{0},\dots,\psi_{m}
be another collection of vectors in
\mathcal{H}
such that
\psi_{0}=\psi^{\prime}_{0}
and

\bigl\lVert\psi_{t}-U_{i}\psi_{t-1}\bigr\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{i}

(4.13)

for all
i
.
Then,

\bigl\lVert\psi_{m}-\psi^{\prime}_{m}\bigr\rVert\leq\sum_{t=1}^{m}\varepsilon_{t}.

(4.14)

Proof.

Denote by
V_{t}
the product
U_{m}U_{m-1}\cdots U_{t+1}
.
In particular,
V_{m}=I
.
Then,

\psi_{m}-\psi^{\prime}_{m}=V_{m}\psi_{m}-V_{0}\psi_{0}=\sum_{t=1}^{m}\bigl(V_{t}\psi_{t}-V_{t-1}\psi_{t-1}\bigr)=\sum_{t=1}^{m}V_{t}\bigl(\psi_{t}-U_{t}\psi_{t-1}\bigr).

We obtain (
4.14
) from the triangle inequality using (
4.13
) and the unitarity of
V_{t}
.
∎

In the application of this lemma,
U_{t}
stands for sequential sections of a quantum algorithm.
The vectors
\psi^{\prime}_{t}
form the sequence of states the algorithm goes through during its execution.
The vectors
\psi_{t}
is an idealised sequence, which is used instead of
\psi_{t}^{\prime}
in the analysis.

We call the difference between
\psi_{t}
and
U_{t}\psi_{t-1}
a (conceptual) perturbation.
The expression in (
4.13
) is the size of the perturbation.
Therefore, the Eq. (
4.14
) can be stated as the total perturbation of the algorithm does not exceed the sum of the perturbations of individual steps.
If this sum is small, the final state
\psi_{m}
of the analysis is not too far away from the actual final state
\psi^{\prime}_{m}
of the algorithm.

This lemma is implicitly used every time an approximate version of a quantum subroutine is used, which happens in pretty much every non-trivial quantum algorithm.

4.7
Efficient Implementation of Direct-Sum Finite Automata

We will repeatedly use the following construction in this paper, for which we describe a time-efficient implementation.
We call it direct-sum quantum finite automata due to its superficial similarity to quantum finite automata.

The space of the algorithm is
\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{H}
, where
\mathcal{K}
is a
K
-qudit,
\mathcal{P}
is a qubit, and
\mathcal{H}
is an arbitrary space.
Additionally, we have black-box access to
K
unitaries
S_{0},\dots,S_{K-1}
in
\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{H}
.
The algorithm is promised to start in the state of the form

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\phi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}},

(4.15)

and it has to perform the following transformation

•

For
t=0,1,\dots,K-1
:

(a)

Execute
S_{t}
on
\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{H}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

(b)

Conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}
, replace
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t+1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

(4.16)

Let us elaborate on the “replace” in point (b).
It is not hard to show by induction that the
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}
-part of the state contains a vector of the form
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\phi_{t+1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}
.
This vector has to be replaced by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t+1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\phi_{t+1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}
.
We also assume that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|K}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
is identical with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.
For a graphical illustration refer to Figure
4.7
.

S_{0}
\phi_{0}
\psi_{0}
\psi^{\prime}_{0}
S_{1}
\phi_{1}
\psi_{1}
\psi^{\prime}_{1}
S_{2}
\phi_{2}
\psi_{2}
\psi^{\prime}_{2}
S_{3}
\phi_{3}
\psi_{3}
\psi^{\prime}_{3}
\phi_{4}

It is trivial to implement the transformation in (
4.16
) in
\mathcal{O}(K\log K)
elementary operations besides the executions of
S_{t}
.
It is a technical observation that we can remove the logarithmic factor.

Lemma 4.6
.

The transformation in (
4.16
) can be implemented in time
\mathcal{O}(K)+\sum_{t}T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{t})
, where
T_{\mathrm{C}}
is defined in Section
4.4
.

Proof.

The register
\mathcal{K}
uses
\ell=\log K
qubits.
We introduce an additional register
\mathcal{C}
that also consists of
\ell
qubits.
For
i,t\in[K]
, we denote

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i\curlyvee t}}\right>_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes c}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes\ell-c}

if the binary representations of
i
and
t
, considered as elements of
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, agree on the first
c
most significant bits, and disagree on the
(c+1)
-st one (or
c=\ell
).

We modify the algorithm so that before the
t
-th iteration of the loop, the algorithm is in a state of the form

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t\curlyvee t}}\right>_{\mathcal{C}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\phi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i\curlyvee t}}\right>_{\mathcal{C}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi^{\prime}_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{i=t}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i\curlyvee t}}\right>_{\mathcal{C}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}

(4.17)

In particular, in the
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}
-part, the register
\mathcal{C}
contains
\ell
ones.

The state (
4.17
) with
t=0
can be obtained from (
4.15
) in
\mathcal{O}(\ell)
elementary operations by computing
\mathcal{C}
starting from the highest qubit.
Execution of
S_{t}
on Step (a) can be conditioned on the lowest qubit of
\mathcal{C}
, which is equal to 1 if and only if
\mathcal{K}
contains
t
.

It remains to consider Step (b) and the update of the register
\mathcal{C}
during the increment from
t
to
t+1
.
Assume that
t+1
is divisible by
2^{d}
, but not by
2^{d+1}
.
Then, Step (b) takes
d+1
controlled 1-qubit operations.
Similarly, the change from
t
to
t+1
in
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i\curlyvee t}}\right>_{\mathcal{C}}
in (
4.17
) takes time
\mathcal{O}(d)
by first uncomputing the
d+1
lowest qubits for
t
, and then computing them for
t+1
.
Finally, after the loop, the register
\mathcal{C}
can be uncomputed in
\mathcal{O}(\ell)
operations.
Therefore, the total number of elementary operations is
\mathcal{O}(K)
.
∎

5
Transducers

In this section, we define transducers and give their basic properties.
This is an initial treatment and will be extended in Section
7
to include query complexity.

5.1
Definition

Mathematically, our approach is based on the following construction.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
be a direct sum of two vector spaces, and
S
be a unitary on
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
.
Then, for every
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, there exist
\tau\in\mathcal{H}
and
v\in\mathcal{L}
such that

S\colon\xi\oplus v\mapsto\tau\oplus v.

(5.1)

Moreover,

(a)

The vector
\tau=\tau(S,\xi)=\tau_{\mathcal{H}}(S,\xi)
is uniquely defined by
\xi
and
S
.

(b)

The vector
v=v(S,\xi)=v_{\mathcal{H}}(S,\xi)
is also uniquely defined if we require that it is orthogonal to the 1-eigenspace of
\Pi S\Pi
, where
\Pi
is the projection on
\mathcal{L}
.

(c)

The mapping
\xi\mapsto\tau
is unitary and
\xi\mapsto v
is linear.

We will prove the theorem at the end of this section.

In the setting of Theorem
5.1
, we will say that
S

transduces

\xi
into
\tau
, and write
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
.
The mapping
\xi\mapsto\tau
on
\mathcal{H}
will be called the
transduction action
of
S
on
\mathcal{H}
and denoted by
S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}}
.

We call any
v
satisfying
S(\xi\oplus v)=\tau\oplus v
a
catalyst
for
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
.
The condition in Point (b) of
v
to be orthogonal to the 1-eigenspace of
\Pi S\Pi
is crucial for uniqueness, as adding such a vector to
v
does not affect (
5.1
).
Clearly, the vector
v
as defined in Point (b) has the smallest possible norm.
Therefore, we can define transduction complexity of
S
on
\xi
as
W(S,\xi)=\|v(S,\xi)\|^{2}
for the latter
v
.
We write
W_{\mathcal{H}}(S,\xi)
if the space
\mathcal{H}
might not be clear from the context.
The above discussion can be formulated as the following claim.

Claim 5.2
.

For any catalyst
v
of the transduction
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
, we have
W(S,\xi)\leq\|v\|^{2}
.

On the other hand, checking orthogonality to
\Pi S\Pi
is complicated and unnecessary, and we avoid doing it.
We usually couple a transducer with some chosen catalyst
v
for all the
\xi
of interest, which need not have the smallest possible norm.
In this case, we somewhat sloppily write
W(S,\xi)=\|v\|^{2}
even for this catalyst
v
.
This agreement will become especially important when we add query complexity into the picture in Section
7
; see in particular the note towards the end of Section
7.1
.

Other important notions related to the transducer are its time and query complexity.
Its time complexity,
T(S)
, is defined as its time complexity as an algorithm.
For
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, its query state,
q_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi)
, and query complexity,
L_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi)
, are defined as those of
S
as an algorithm on the initial state
\xi\oplus v
.
Note that for transducers with input oracles, we will adopt a special canonical form defined in Section
7
, until then we mostly ignore the oracle-related notions.

Example 5.3
.

Let us a give a simple concrete example illustrating the above notions.
Assume that
\mathcal{H}
is one-dimensional and spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
, and
\mathcal{L}
is two-dimensional and spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
Let
S
be the reflection of the vector
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
, so that its orthogonal complement stays intact.

The transduction action of
S
on
\mathcal{H}
is the identity, which is certified by

S\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

Hence, we have

v(S,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})/2
,
and

W(S,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=1/2
.
This is not the only catalyst, as one can also take
v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
or
v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
However,
(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})/2
is the only catalyst orthogonal to the 1-eigenspace of
\Pi S\Pi
, which is spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
, and also has the smallest norm.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

The vector
v
can be found from the equation

\Pi v=\Pi\bigl(\tau+v\bigr)=\Pi\bigl(S(\xi+v)\bigr)=\Pi S\xi+\Pi Sv=\Pi S\xi+\Pi S\Pi v.

From this we would like to argue that
v
can be expressed as

v=(\Pi-\Pi S\Pi)^{+}\Pi S\xi,

(5.2)

where
(\cdot)^{+}
stands for the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse.
Let us show that this is indeed the case.

Denote by
\mathcal{K}
the kernel of
\Pi-\Pi S\Pi
in
\mathcal{L}
, and by
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
its orthogonal complement in
\mathcal{L}
.
The subspace
\mathcal{K}
equals the 1-eigenspace of
\Pi S\Pi
.
But since
S
is a unitary, a 1-eigenvector of
\Pi S\Pi
is necessarily a 1-eigenvector of
S
.
Hence,
S
is a direct sum of the identity on
\mathcal{K}
and a unitary on
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
Thus,
\Pi-\Pi S\Pi
is a direct sum of the zero operator in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{K}
and some operator in
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
Moreover, the latter operator is invertible in
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
as its kernel is empty.
Since
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{K}
, we get that
\Pi S\xi\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
Hence, (
5.2
) indeed uniquely specifies
v
.
This proves (b) and the second half of (c).

The uniqueness of
\tau
and the linearity of
\xi\mapsto\tau
now follow from (
5.1
) and the linearity of
S
.
Finally, unitarity of
S
implies
\|\xi\|=\|\tau\|
, hence, the map
\xi\mapsto\tau
is also unitary.
∎

Proposition 5.4

(Transitivity of Transduction)
.

Assume
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{2}
are vector spaces, and
S
is a unitary in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
.
Then,

S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}}=(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}})\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}},

and, for every
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
, a possible catalyst is

v_{\mathcal{H}}(S,\xi)=v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)+v_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\bigl(S,\xi\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)\bigr).

(5.3)

Proof.

By definition,

S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\colon\xi\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)\mapsto\tau\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)

for some
\tau\in\mathcal{H}
. The latter means that

S\colon\xi\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\bigl(S,\xi\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)\bigr)\mapsto\tau\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\bigl(S,\xi\oplus v_{\mathcal{H}}(S\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\xi)\bigr),

proving (
5.3
).
∎

5.2
Implementation

The key point we will now make
is that given a transducer
S
, there exists a very simple quantum algorithm that approximately implements its transduction action on
\mathcal{H}
.
This algorithm is a generalisation of the one from
[
17
]
, which was used for implementation of the adversary bound.

Before we describe this algorithm, let us establish a few conventions.
We call
\mathcal{H}
the
public
and
\mathcal{L}
the
private
space of
S
.
We indicate this separation of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{L}
by a privacy qubit
\mathcal{P}
.
The value 0 of
\mathcal{P}
will indicate the public space
\mathcal{H}
,
and the value 1 the private space
\mathcal{L}
.
This means that both
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{L}
are embedded into the same register during implementation.
Thus,

\xi\oplus v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}

(5.4)

explicitly specifying the public and the private spaces.
We will extend this notation in Section
7.1
.

As it can be understood from the name, the algorithm does not have direct access to the private space
\mathcal{L}
of the transducer. All the interaction between the transducer and its surrounding is through the public space
\mathcal{H}
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let spaces
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{L}
, and a positive integer
K
be fixed.
There exists a quantum algorithm that transforms
\xi
into
\tau^{\prime}
such that

\|\tau^{\prime}-\tau(S,\xi)\|\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{W(S,\xi)}{K}}

for every transducer
S\colon\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
and initial state
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
.
The algorithm conditionally executes
S
as a black box
K
times, and uses
\mathcal{O}(K)
other elementary operations.

Theorem
3.2
is a direct corollary of Theorem
5.5
.
A sketch of the proof of Theorem
5.5
was already given in the same section, see, in particular, Figure
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

The space of the algorithm is
\mathcal{K}\otimes(\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L})
, where
\mathcal{K}
is a
K
-qudit.
The register
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
contains the privacy qubit
\mathcal{P}
as described above. The algorithm starts in the state
\xi=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}
, and
performs the following transformations:

1.

Map
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
into the uniform superposition
\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

2.

For
t=0,1,\dots,K-1
:

(a)

Execute
S
on

\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}

conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

(b)

Conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}
, replace
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t+1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
(where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|K}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
is equal to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
).

3.

Run Step 1 in reverse.

Clearly, the algorithm conditionally executes
S
exactly
K
times.
As described now, the algorithm takes time
\mathcal{O}(K\log K)
, but it is implementable in time
\mathcal{O}(K)
using Lemma
4.6
.

Let us prove correctness.
We write
v=v(S,\xi)
and
\tau=\tau(S,\xi)
.
After Step 1, the algorithm is in the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}.

(5.5)

We perform a perturbation in the sense of Lemma
4.5
and assume the algorithm is instead in the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}.

(5.6)

On the
t
-th iteration of the loop, the transducer
S
on Step 2(a) transforms the part of the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\;\longmapsto\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}},

(5.7)

and on Step 2(b) the following transformation of the part of the state is performed:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\;\longmapsto\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t+1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}.

Therefore, after the execution of the loop in Step 2, we get the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}.

(5.8)

We perturb the state to

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}.

(5.9)

After Step 3, we get the state
\tau=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Note that the difference between the states in (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) has norm
\|v\|/\sqrt{K}
.
The same is true for the difference between the states in (
5.8
) and (
5.9
).
Therefore, by Lemma
4.5
, the actual final state
\tau^{\prime}
of the algorithm satisfies

\|\tau^{\prime}-\tau\|\leq 2\frac{\|v\|}{\sqrt{K}}

as required.
∎

6
Example I: Quantum Walks

In this section, we implement the electric quantum walk from
[
12
]
using the construction outlined in Section
3.2
.
See also a subsequent paper
[
15
]
, where similar ideas are applied to search and to the Welded Tree problem
[
21
]
.

A
u_{2}
u_{1}
B
w_{e}
u_{2}^{\prime}
u_{1}^{\prime}
\sigma_{1}
\sigma_{2}

A quantum walk is described by a bipartite graph
G
, whose parts we denote by
A
and
B
.
Let
E
be the set of edges of
G
.
Each edge
e
of the graph is given a non-negative real
weight

w_{e}
.
We have some set
M\subseteq A\cup B
of
marked
vertices.
There is a subroutine
Check
that, for every vertex
u
, says whether it is marked.
The goal of the quantum walk is to detect whether
M
is empty or not.

In the framework of electric quantum walks, the graph is extended as follows, see Figure
6
.
The quantum walk is tailored towards a specific initial probability distribution
\sigma
on
A
.
Let
A_{\sigma}\subseteq A
be the support of
\sigma
.
For each
u\in A_{\sigma}
, we add a new vertex
u^{\prime}
and a new dangling edge
u^{\prime}u
to the graph.
The newly added vertices are
not
contained in
B
.
Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of newly added edges.
For edges if
E^{\prime}
we assume the weight
w_{u^{\prime}u}=\sigma_{u}
.

We treat this construction as a transducer.
The private space
\mathcal{L}
of the quantum walk is
\mathbb{C}^{E}
.
The public space
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathbb{C}^{E^{\prime}}
.
The initial state
\xi
is given by

\xi=\sum_{u\in A_{\sigma}}\sqrt{\sigma_{u}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|u^{\prime}u}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\in\mathcal{H}.

For a vertex
u
, let
\mathcal{L}_{u}
denote the space spanned by all the edges incident to
u
(including the ones in
E^{\prime}
).
Define

\psi_{u}=\sum_{e:e\sim u}\sqrt{w_{e}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\in\mathcal{L}_{u}

(6.1)

where the sum is over all the edges incident to
u
.

For
U\subseteq A
or
U\subseteq B
, let
R_{U}
denote the reflection of all
\psi_{u}
for
u\in U
, i.e.,
R_{U}
acts as negation on the span of all these
\psi_{u}
and as identity on its orthogonal complement.
We define the transducer, which depends on the set of marked vertices
M
, as

S_{M}=R_{B\setminus M}R_{A\setminus M}.

Each of
R_{A\setminus M}
and
R_{B\setminus M}
is decomposable into products of local reflections in
\mathcal{L}_{u}
as
u
ranges over
A
and
B
, respectively.
The corresponding local reflections are either identities for
u\in M
, or reflections of
\psi_{u}
for
u\notin M
.
The implementation of
S_{M}
can be done using local reflections controlled by the
Check
subroutine.

Let

W=\sum_{e\in E}w_{e}

be the total weight of the graph.
For
M\neq\emptyset
, let
R_{\sigma,M}
be the minimum of

\sum_{e\in E}\frac{p_{e}^{2}}{w_{e}},

(6.2)

over all flows
p=(p_{e})
on the graph where
\sigma_{u}
units of flow are injected in
u\in A_{\sigma}
, and the flow is collected at the vertices in
M
.
The minimum is attained by the electrical flow, and
R_{\sigma,M}
is the corresponding effective resistance.

Theorem 6.1
.

The transducer
S_{M}
defined above transduces
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}-\xi
if
M=\emptyset
, and
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\xi
otherwise.
Its transduction complexity is

W\bigl(S_{\emptyset},\xi\bigr)=W\qquad\text{and}\qquad W\bigl(S_{M},\xi\bigr)=R_{\sigma,M}

(6.3)

respectively.

Thus, the transduction action of the quantum walk encodes the answer to the detection problem in the phase, and this is done exactly.
Let
R
denote the maximal effective resistance over all possible choices of
M\neq\emptyset
.
We can rescale all
w_{i}
by the same factor so that the maximal transduction complexity in (
6.3
) becomes equal to
\sqrt{RW}
.
By Theorem
3.2
, the presence of marked elements can be detected, with bounded error, in
O(\sqrt{RW})
executions of
S_{M}
.
This coincides with the complexity estimate established in
[
12
]
.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Let us start with the case
M=\emptyset
.
The initial coupling is

\xi\oplus v_{\emptyset}=\sum_{e\in E\cup E^{\prime}}\sqrt{w_{e}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

We have
\xi\oplus v_{\emptyset}=\sum_{u\in A}\psi_{u}
and
v_{\emptyset}=\sum_{u\in B}\psi_{u}
.
Hence,
R_{A}
reflects all
\xi\oplus v_{\emptyset}
, and
R_{B}
only reflects
v_{\emptyset}
.
This gives us the following chain of transformations

\xi\oplus v_{\emptyset}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{A}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\xi\oplus-v_{\emptyset}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R_{B}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;-\xi\oplus v_{\emptyset},

giving
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{\emptyset}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}-\xi
.
Note that this chain of transformation adheres to (
3.5
).

Now assume
M\neq\emptyset
.
Let
p_{e}
be a flow on the graph where
\sigma_{u}
units of flow are injected in
u^{\prime}
, and the flow is collected at
M
.
To solve the sign ambiguity, we assume that all the edges are oriented towards
A
.
This time, we define the catalyst
v_{M}
so that

\xi\oplus v_{M}=\sum_{e\in E\cup E^{\prime}}\frac{p_{e}}{\sqrt{w_{e}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

(6.4)

Recall that
p_{u^{\prime}u}=w_{u^{\prime}u}=\sigma_{u}
, hence the above equation is satisfied in
\mathcal{H}
.

The projection of (
6.4
) onto
\mathcal{L}_{u}
is given by

\sum_{e:e\sim u}\frac{p_{e}}{\sqrt{w_{e}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|e}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

(6.5)

This state is not changed by the corresponding local reflection in
\mathcal{L}_{u}
.
Indeed if
u\in M
, the corresponding local reflection is the identity.
If
u\notin M
, then, by the flow condition, the state in (
6.5
) is orthogonal to
\psi_{u}
in (
6.1
).
Thus, neither
R_{A\setminus M}
nor
R_{B\setminus M}
change
\xi\oplus v_{M}
, hence,
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{M}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\xi
.
Note that in this case we adhere to (
3.4
).

The corresponding transduction complexities are as given in (
6.3
).
The catalyst in the second case uses the flow
p_{e}
through the graph, which is not unique.
We choose the minimal one as per Claim
5.2
.
∎

7
Canonical Transducers

In this section, we define a specific form of transducers we will be using in this paper.
The main point is in the application of the input oracle.
Inspired by the construction in
[
17
]
, we assume that the transducer first executes the input oracle, and then performs some input-independent unitary.
Moreover, the input oracle is always applied to the private space of the transducer.
These assumptions simplify many constructions, and any transducer can be transformed into the canonical form with a small overhead as shown later in Proposition
10.4
.

7.1
Definition

Concerning the input oracle, the assumptions are similar to those in Section
4.1
.
The input oracle is a unitary
O
in some space
\mathcal{M}
, and we have unidirectional access to
O
.
The oracle only acts on the local space
\mathcal{L}
of the transducer.
Let us decompose the latter in two parts
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
, which stand for the work (non-query) and query parts.
We also have
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\otimes\mathcal{M}
for some space
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
.
We denote the identity on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
simply by
I
.

A canonical transducer
S=S(O)
performs the following transformations, see also Figure
3.3
:

•

It executes the input oracle
I\otimes O
on
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
.
Similarly to (
4.1
), we call it a query and denote it by

\widetilde{O}=I_{\mathcal{H}}\oplus I^{\circ}\oplus I\otimes O,

(7.1)

where
I_{\mathcal{H}}
and
I^{\circ}
are identities on
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}
, respectively.

•

It performs an input-independent work unitary
S^{\circ}
on
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
.

The decomposition
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
yields the decomposition
v=v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}
of the catalyst.
Thus, the action
S(O)
of the transducer
S
on the input oracle
O
is given by the following chain of transformations:

S(O)\colon\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S^{\circ}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\tau\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}.

(7.2)

Now we can make the following complexity-related definitions.
The catalyst is
v=v(S,O,\xi)
.
11
11

11

It is the same as
v(S(O),\xi)
in the previous notation, however, we opted to
v(S,O,\xi)
to reduce the number of brackets and to keep notation synchronised with
[
17
]
.

The transduction complexity is

W(S,O,\xi)=\bigl\lVert v(S,O,\xi)\bigr\rVert^{2}.

(7.3)

The query state is
q(S,O,\xi)=v^{\bullet}=\Pi^{\bullet}v(S,O,\xi)
, where
\Pi^{\bullet}
denotes the orthogonal projector onto
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
.
The (Las Vegas) query complexity of the transducer is

L(S,O,\xi)=\bigl\lVert q(S,O,\xi)\bigr\rVert^{2}.

(7.4)

Note that formally the definitions
q(S,O,\xi)
and
L(S,O,\xi)
are in conflict with the same definitions (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) if
S
is considered as a
program
and not as a transducer.
However, this should not cause a confusion.
If the space
\mathcal{H}
is not clear from the context, we will add it as a subscript as in Section
5.1
.

Time complexity
T(S)
of the transducer is the number of elementary operations required to implement the unitary
S^{\circ}
.
Note that we do not count the query towards time complexity of the transducer.

Finally, the definitions (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) can be extended to
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{H}
using (
3.6
).

Note on Non-Uniqueness of Catalyst

It is important to note that in this setting the non-uniqueness of the catalyst
v
discussed in Section
5.1
becomes very important.
To understand why, consider again Example
5.3
from that section.
This time, assume that
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}
is spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
, the input oracle is
O=I
, and
S^{\circ}=S
as defined previously.

The “right” catalyst
v=(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})/2
for the initial state
\xi=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
suggests that
W(S,O,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=1/2
and
L(S,O,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=1/4
.
However, if we take
v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
, we get that
W(S,O,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=1
and
L(S,O,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=0
.
Therefore, there is no longer a single catalyst that minimises both the transduction and the query complexity.
This is similar to usual algorithms, where time and query complexity can be minimised by different algorithms.

We solve this complication by implicitly assigning a specific catalyst
v(S,O,\xi)
for every
O
and
\xi
of interest, that gives
both

W(S,O,\xi)
and
L(S,O,\xi)
simultaneously.
Of course, neither of the two are guaranteed to be minimal.
It is possible to study the trade-off between the transduction and the query complexity for a fixed
O
and
\xi
, but we will not explicitly pursue that in this paper.

Implementation Details

In terms of registers, as in Section
4.1
, the separation
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
is indicated by the qubit
\mathcal{R}
.
Now it makes sense to assume that the registers
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
are the same, the distinction being given by the values of the registers
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{R}
.
We will usually place this common register as the unnamed last register in our expressions.
In particular,

\xi\oplus v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

(7.5)

Note that the space
\mathcal{H}
is indicated by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
meaning that it is not acted on by the oracle.
This allows us to implement the query as an application of
O
controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
,
which is in accord with the convention established in Section
4.1
.

We will also use registers
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{L}
in the sense of Section
5.2
, that is, containing
\mathcal{R}
.
In particular, we can write the action of a canonical transducer (
7.2
) in registers like

S(O)\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl|\widetilde{O}v\bigr>_{\mathcal{L}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S^{\circ}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}},

(7.6)

where we used shorthand

\widetilde{O}v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}.

(7.7)

7.2
Multiple Input Oracles

Following
[
17
]
, we can also allow multiple input oracles joined by direct sum:

O=O^{(1)}\oplus O^{(2)}\oplus\cdots\oplus O^{(r)},

(7.8)

where the
i
-th input oracle
O^{(i)}
acts in space
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}
and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{(1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{M}^{(r)}
.
We get the corresponding decomposition of the query state:

q(S,O,\xi)=q^{(1)}(S,O,\xi)\oplus\cdots\oplus q^{(r)}(S,O,\xi),

(7.9)

where
q^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)
is the
partial query state
of the
i
-th input oracle.
This also gives query complexities of the individual oracles:

L^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)=\bigl\lVert q^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)\bigr\rVert^{2}.

It makes sense to tweak the notation assumed earlier in Section
7.1
to make it in line with Section
4.2
.
We assume the register
\mathcal{R}
can hold an integer from 0 to
r
, where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
with
i>0
indicates the space of the
i
-th input oracle.
Thus, in place of (
7.5
), we have

\xi\oplus v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}

(7.10)

with
v^{(i)}=q^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)
.
To apply the
i
-th input oracle
O^{(i)}
, it suffices to condition it on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.
The action of a canonical transducer stays given by (
7.6
), where, this time,

\widetilde{O}v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\bigl(I\otimes O^{(i)}\bigr)v^{(i)}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

(7.11)

For notational convenience, we will assume a single input oracle in most of the paper.
The case of multiple oracles can be obtained using the decomposition of the query state in (
7.9
), which contains all the necessary information.

7.3
Reducing the Number of Oracle Calls

One problem with the algorithm in Theorem
5.5
is that, when applied to a canonical transducer, the input oracle
O
is executed the same number of times as the work unitary
S^{\circ}
.
This is suboptimal as the transduction complexity can be much larger than the query complexity.
In this section, we describe a query-efficient implementation, which can also handle multiple input oracles.

Let
S
be a canonical transducer with
r
input oracles joined into one oracle
O
via direct sum as in (
7.8
).
In the following theorem, we assume the spaces
\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
,
\mathcal{M}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{M}^{(r)}
are fixed, while the operators
S^{\circ}
and
O^{(1)},\dots,O^{(r)}
can vary.

Theorem 7.1

(Query-Optimal Implementation of Transducers)
.

Let
K\geq K^{(1)},\dots,K^{(r)}
be positive integers, which we assume to be powers of 2 for simplicity.
There exists a quantum algorithm that conditionally executes
S^{\circ}
as a black box
K
times, makes
K^{(i)}
queries to the
i
-th input oracle
O^{(i)}
, and uses
\mathcal{O}(K+K^{(1)}+\cdots+K^{(r)})\log r
other elementary operations.
For each
S^{\circ}
,
O^{(i)}
, and initial state
\xi
, the algorithm transforms
\xi
into
\tau^{\prime}
such that

\bigl\lVert\tau^{\prime}-\tau(S,O,\xi)\bigr\rVert\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{K}}\sqrt{W(S,O,\xi)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{K}{K^{(i)}}-1}}\right)L^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)}.

(7.12)

Theorem
5.5
is a special case of this theorem with all
K^{(i)}
equal to
K
