.
Observe that the number of elementary operations is equal to the total number of invocations of
S^{\circ}
and
O^{(i)}
times
\log r
. It is highly unlikely that this part of the algorithm would dominate its time complexity.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

The proof is an extension of that of Theorem
5.5
.
Its outline was already given in Section
3.3
; see in particular Figure
3.3
.

Let us define
D^{(i)}=K/K^{(i)}
, which is a power of 2.
The space of the algorithm is
\mathcal{K}\otimes(\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L})
, where
\mathcal{K}
is a
K
-qudit.
The register
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
contains the registers
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{R}
as described above.
The algorithm starts its work in the state
\xi=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
Its steps are as follows.

1.

Map
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
into the uniform superposition
\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

2.

For
t=0,1,\dots,K-1
:

(a)

For each
i=1,\dots,r
:

•

if
t
is divisible by
D^{(i)}
, execute the input oracle
O^{(i)}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.

(b)

Execute the work unitary
S^{\circ}
on
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

(c)

Conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
, replace
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t+1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

(d)

For each
i=1,\dots,r
:

•

if
t+1
is divisible by
D^{(i)}
, add
D^{(i)}
to
\mathcal{K}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
. The operation is performed modulo
K
.

3.

Run Step 1 in reverse.

The analysis is similar to that in the proof of Theorem
5.5
.
Now we assume that after Step 1, instead of the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
,
we are in the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{t=0}^{D^{(i)}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\Bigr].

(7.13)

Since
t=0
is divisible by all
D^{(i)}
, after Step 2(a) of the first iteration of the loop, we have the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{t=0}^{D^{(i)}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|(I\otimes O^{(i)})v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\Bigr].

The crucial observation is that on each iteration of the loop on step 2(b), the following transformation is performed.
The part of the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|(I\otimes O^{(i)})v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\Bigr]

(7.14)

gets mapped by
S^{\circ}
into

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\Bigr],

(7.15)

where we used (
7.6
) with (
7.11
).

If
t=cD^{(i)}-1
for some integer
c
, then on Step 2(d), we perform the transformation of the part of the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\sum_{t=0}^{D^{(i)}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(c-1)D^{(i)}+t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\bigl|v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\sum_{t=0}^{D^{(i)}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|cD^{(i)}+t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\bigl|v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}},

(7.16)

which is then mapped on Step 2(a) of the next iteration into

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\sum_{t=0}^{D^{(i)}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|cD^{(i)}+t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(I\otimes O^{(i)})v^{(i)}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

Therefore, after all the
K
iterations of the loop in Step 2, we result in the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{t=0}^{D^{(i)}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\Bigr].

After that, we finish as in Theorem
5.5
, by assuming we are in the state

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{t=0}^{K-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}

instead, and applying Step 3.

The total perturbation of the algorithm is

\frac{2}{\sqrt{K}}\Bigl\lVert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{t=0}^{D^{(i)}-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\bigl|v^{(i)}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\Bigr\rVert,

(7.17)

which is equal to the right-hand side of (
7.12
).

The claim on the number of executions of
S^{\circ}
and
O^{(i)}
in the algorithm is obvious.
Besides that, it is trivial to implement the algorithm in

\mathcal{O}(K+K^{(1)}+\cdots+K^{(r)})\log K\log r
elementary operations,
where the
\log r
factors comes from the necessity to index one of the
r
input oracles.
Using a slight modification of Lemma
4.6
, the algorithm can be implemented in
\mathcal{O}(K+K^{(1)}+\cdots+K^{(r)})\log r
elementary operations.
One crucial point here is that when
t=cD^{(i)}-1
, addition of
D^{(i)}
on Step 2(d) is equivalent to the replacement of
c-1
by
c
in the highest qubits of the register
\mathcal{K}
as indicated by (
7.16
).
We omit the details.
∎

The following corollary, which is equivalent to Theorem
3.3
, is easier to apply.

Corollary 7.2
.

Assume
r=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and let
\varepsilon,W,L^{(1)},\dots,L^{(r)}>0
be parameters.
There exists a quantum algorithm that conditionally executes
S^{\circ}
as a black box
K=\mathcal{O}(1+W/\varepsilon^{2})
times,
makes
\mathcal{O}(L^{(i)}/\varepsilon^{2})
queries to the
i
-th input oracle
O^{(i)}
, and uses
\mathcal{O}(K)
other elementary operations.
The algorithm
\varepsilon
-approximately transforms
\xi
into
\tau(S,O,\xi)
for all
S
,
O^{(i)}
, and
\xi
such that
W(S,O,\xi)\leq W
and
L^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)\leq L^{(i)}
for all
i
.

Proof of Corollary
7.2
.

We first prove a relaxed version of the corollary, where we allow each input oracle to be called
\mathcal{O}\bigl(1+L^{(i)}/\varepsilon^{2}\bigr)
times.
Afterwards, we show how to remove this assumption.
We allow error
\varepsilon/2
in this relaxed version.

If
W<\varepsilon^{2}/16
, the (relaxed) corollary follows from Theorem
5.5
, as the algorithm then executes
S^{\circ}
and each input oracle
K=1
times.
Therefore, we will assume
W\geq\varepsilon^{2}/16
.
Also, by definition we have that
W(S,O,\xi)\geq L^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)
.
Therefore, we may assume that
W\geq L^{(i)}
, reducing
L^{(i)}
otherwise.

We intend to use Theorem
7.1
.
We take
K
as the smallest power of 2 exceeding
32(r+1)W/\varepsilon^{2}
.
In particular,
K=\Theta(W/\varepsilon^{2})
by our assumption on
W
and
r=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
We take
K^{(i)}
as the largest power of 2 that does not exceed
\max\bigl\{1,\,KL^{(i)}/W\bigr\}
.
It satisfies
K^{(i)}\leq K
as required.
On the other hand,
K^{(i)}\geq KL^{(i)}/(2W)
implying
K/K^{(i)}\leq 2W/L^{(i)}
, which gives the error estimate (
7.12
) at most

\frac{2}{\sqrt{K}}\sqrt{2(r+1)W}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2(r+1)W}}{\sqrt{32(r+1)W/\varepsilon^{2}}}\leq\varepsilon/2.

If
K^{(i)}>1
, we get
K^{(i)}\leq KL^{(i)}/W=\mathcal{O}(L^{(i)}/\varepsilon^{2})
, which finishes the proof of the relaxed statement of the corollary.

In the following, we assume we used Theorem
7.1
in the proof, the case of Theorem
5.5
being similar.
Consider all the values of
i
such that
K^{(i)}=1
.
By the proof of Theorem
7.1
, the input oracles are applied to the perturbation added in (
7.13
), also the norm of the perturbation is at most
\varepsilon/4
(
cf.
(
7.17
)).

To get the original statement of the corollary, we do not apply all the input oracles
O^{(i)}
with
K^{(i)}=1
.
As they are applied once in the algorithm, this gives additional perturbation of size at most
\varepsilon/2
.
Combining with the perturbation
\varepsilon/2
of the relaxed algorithm itself, we get an estimate of the total error of at most
\varepsilon
.
∎

8
Example II: Adversary Bound

As mentioned in the introduction, the construction of transducers is based on the implementation of the adversary bound in
[
17
]
.
The quantum adversary bound was first developed as a powerful tool for proving quantum query lower bounds.
However, it was later extended to include upper bounds as well, and we consider the latter in this paper.
For more detail on the adversary bound, refer to the introduction of
[
17
]
and the references therein.
We first consider the general case of state conversion with unidirectional unitary input oracles, and then move on to more usual function evaluation problems.

8.1
State Conversion

We consider the adversary bound for state conversion from
[
17
]
.
In the state conversion problem, we have a collection of pairs
\xi_{x}\mapsto\tau_{x}
of states in
\mathcal{H}
and input oracles
O_{x}\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
, where
x
ranges over some finite set
D
.
The task is to develop an algorithm
A
such that
A(O_{x})\xi_{x}=\tau_{x}
for all
x
.
The goal is to minimise
L(A,O_{x},\xi_{x})
.
The corresponding adversary bound is the following multi-objective optimisation problem:

minimise

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v_{x}}}\right\rVert^{2}\bigr)_{x\in D}

(8.1a)

subject to

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\xi_{x},\xi_{y}}}\right\rangle-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\tau_{x},\tau_{y}}}\right\rangle=\bigl\langle v_{x},\;(I_{\mathcal{W}}\otimes(I_{\mathcal{M}}-O^{*}_{x}O_{y}))v_{y}\bigr\rangle

for all
x,y\in D
;

(8.1b)

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{W}$ is a vector space},\qquad v_{x}\in\mathcal{W}\otimes\mathcal{M}.

(8.1c)

A canonical transducer
S_{v}
can be obtained from any feasible solution
v=(v_{x})
to this problem.
It works as follows (with
I=I_{\mathcal{W}}
):

\xi_{x}\oplus v_{x}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I\otimes O_{x}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\xi_{x}\oplus(I\otimes O_{x})v_{x}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S^{\circ}_{v}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\tau_{x}\oplus v_{x},

where
S^{\circ}_{v}
is an input-independent unitary whose existence is assured by (
8.1b
), as the latter can be rewritten as

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\xi_{x},\xi_{y}}}\right\rangle+\bigl\langle(I\otimes O_{x})v_{x},\;(I\otimes O_{y})v_{y}\bigr\rangle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\tau_{x},\tau_{y}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{x},v_{y}}}\right\rangle,

and two state collections with the same combination of inner products always admit such a state-independent transforming unitary.
Thus we have a transduction
\xi_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{v}(O_{x})}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau_{x}
with
the transduction and the query complexities satisfying

W(S_{v},O_{x},\xi_{x})=L(S_{v},O_{x},\xi_{x})=\|v_{x}\|^{2},\qquad q(S_{v},O_{x},\xi_{x})=v_{x}.

As shown in
[
17
]
, this perfectly captures Las Vegas query complexity of state conversion.
Note that canonical transducers with empty non-query space
\mathcal{L}^{\circ}
are essentially equivalent to this construction.

8.2
Function Evaluation

Now we describe the usual case of function evaluation.
These results can be derived from
[
17
]
and
[
14
]
.
First, we define the formalism behind function-evaluating transducers, and then move on to the adversary bound.

Let
f\colon D\to[p]
be a function with domain
D\subseteq[q]^{n}
.
We want to construct a transducer
S_{f}
that evaluates
f
.
We assume the state-generating settings from Section
4.3
, which means
that, for every
x\in D
, with bidirectional access to the input oracle

O_{x}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
,
the transducer
S_{f}
has to perform the transduction
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
Again, we cast this as unidirectional access to
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
from (
4.3
).

Similarly to (
4.10
), we write

W_{x}(S)=W\bigl(S,\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\qquad\text{and}\qquad W(S)=\max_{x\in D}W_{x}(S).

(8.2)

We use similar notation for
L
and
L^{(i)}
.

There is a slight discrepancy between various existing definitions of the adversary bound
\mathop{\mathrm{Adv}^{\pm}}(f)
for non-Boolean functions, in the sense that they differ by a factor of at most 2 (see, e.g., Section 3 of
[
30
]
.)
We adopt the formulation from
[
14
]
, which reads in notation of that paper as

\mathop{\mathrm{Adv}^{\pm}}(f)=\gamma_{2}\Bigl(1_{f(x)\neq f(y)}\mathbin{\bigl|}\bigoplus\nolimits_{i\in[n]}1_{x_{i}\neq y_{i}}\Bigr)_{x,y\in D},

and which is equivalent to
\gamma_{2}(J-F|\Delta)
in notations of
[
30
]
.
An explicit definition of
\mathop{\mathrm{Adv}^{\pm}}(f)
is:
12
12

12

The usual definition has
\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{x,i}\|^{2},\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|v_{x,i}\|^{2}}}\right\}
in the objective instead of
\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{x,i}\|^{2}+\|v_{x,i}\|^{2}\Bigr)
.
The two formulations are equivalent
[
17
]
.
But even a priori, our formulation does not exceed the usual formulation, and, since we are interested in upper bounds, supersedes the latter.

minimise

\displaystyle\max_{x\in D}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl(\|u_{x,i}\|^{2}+\|v_{x,i}\|^{2}\biggr)

(8.3a)

subject to

\displaystyle 1_{f(x)\neq f(y)}=\sum_{i:x_{i}\neq y_{i}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle u_{x,i},v_{y,i}}}\right\rangle

for all
x,y\in D
;

(8.3b)

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{W}$ is a vector space},\qquad u_{x,i},v_{x,i}\in\mathcal{W}.

(8.3c)

Let us now describe the canonical transducer
S_{u,v}
corresponding to a feasible solution
u_{x,i},v_{x,i}
of (
8.3
).
Its local space
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
is of the form
\mathcal{W}\otimes\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{Q}
.
Here
\mathcal{W}
acts as
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
in notation of Section
7.1
,

\mathcal{R}
is an
n
-qudit indicating the index of the input variable,

\mathcal{B}
is a qubit indicating direction of the query,
and
\mathcal{Q}
is a
q
-qudit storing the output of the query.
We use
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
to denote the basis states of
\mathcal{B}
.
The first one stands for the direct, and the second one for the inverse query.
The input oracle acts on
\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{Q}
as

\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}=\bigoplus_{i\in[n]}\overleftrightarrow{O_{x,i}},

(8.4)

where

O_{x,i}\colon\quad\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q},\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}

(8.5)

is the
i
th constituent of the input oracle.

Define the following vectors in
\mathcal{W}
:

v^{\uparrow}_{x,i}=\frac{u_{x,i}+v_{x,i}}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v^{\downarrow}_{x,i}=\frac{u_{x,i}-v_{x,i}}{2}.

They possess the following important property:

\bigl\langle v^{\uparrow}_{x,i},v^{\uparrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle v^{\downarrow}_{x,i},v^{\downarrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle=\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle u_{x,i},v_{y,i}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{x,i},u_{y,i}}}\right\rangle}{2}.

(8.6)

The catalyst for the input
x
is

v_{x}=\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\uparrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl|v^{\uparrow}_{x,i}\bigr>_{\mathcal{W}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\downarrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl|v^{\downarrow}_{x,i}\bigr>_{\mathcal{W}}\Bigr].

(8.7)

The transducer starts in
\xi_{x}\oplus v_{x}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\oplus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{x}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
It applies the input oracle (
8.4
), which gives the state

\psi_{x}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\oplus\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\uparrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl|v^{\uparrow}_{x,i}\bigr>_{\mathcal{W}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\downarrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl|v^{\downarrow}_{x,i}\bigr>_{\mathcal{W}}\Bigr].

(8.8)

The construction then follows from the following claim.

Claim 8.1
.

There exists an input-independent unitary
S^{\circ}_{u,v}
that maps the state
\psi_{x}
from (
8.8
) into
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\oplus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{x}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
for all
x
.

Proof.

Indeed, for a pair of
x,y\in D
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{x},\psi_{y}}}\right\rangle=1+\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigl[1_{x_{i}=y_{i}}\bigl\langle v^{\uparrow}_{x,i},v^{\uparrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle v^{\downarrow}_{x,i},v^{\downarrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle\bigr].

(8.9)

On the other hand, the inner product between

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\oplus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{x}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\oplus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{y}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
is

1_{f(x)=f(y)}+\sum_{i\in[n]}\bigl[\bigl\langle v^{\uparrow}_{x,i},v^{\uparrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle+1_{x_{i}=y_{i}}\bigl\langle v^{\downarrow}_{x,i},v^{\downarrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle\bigr].

(8.10)

To establish the existence of the unitary
S^{\circ}_{u,v}
it suffices to show that (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) are equal for all
x,y\in D
.
Subtracting the latter from the former gives us

1_{f(x)\neq f(y)}-\sum_{i:x_{i}\neq y_{i}}\bigl[\bigl\langle v^{\uparrow}_{x,i},v^{\uparrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle v^{\downarrow}_{x,i},v^{\downarrow}_{y,i}\bigr\rangle\bigr]=1_{f(x)\neq f(y)}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i:x_{i}\neq y_{i}}\bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle u_{x,i},v_{y,i}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{x,i},u_{y,i}}}\right\rangle\bigr]=0

using (
8.6
) and (
8.3b
).
∎

Thus, we have that
S_{u,v}
on the input oracle
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
into
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
If we consider
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
as a direct sum of
n
input oracles as in (
8.4
), we get the partial query states

q^{(i)}_{x}(S_{u,v})=v^{\uparrow}_{x,i}\oplus v^{\downarrow}_{x,i},

with

L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{u,v})=\|v^{\uparrow}_{x,i}\|^{2}+\|v^{\downarrow}_{x,i}\|^{2}=\frac{\|u_{x,i}\|^{2}+\|v_{x,i}\|^{2}}{2}

by the parallelogram identity.
Finally, the transduction complexity and the total query complexity is

W_{x}(S_{u,v})=L_{x}(S_{u,v})=\frac{1}{2}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|u_{x,i}\|^{2}+\|v_{x,i}\|^{2}\biggr)

which is in the objective of (
8.3a
).
This can be summarised as

Theorem 8.2
.

For every function
f\colon D\to[p]
with
D\subseteq[q]^{n}
, there exists a canonical transducer
S_{f}
evaluating the function
f
and whose transduction complexity is bounded by
\mathop{\mathrm{Adv}^{\pm}}(f)
.
In more detail,
the admissible subspace of
S_{f}
is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
;
for every
x\in D
,
S_{f}
transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
with bidirectional access to the input oracle
O_{x}
encoding the input string
x
; and

W_{x}(S_{f})=L_{x}(S_{f})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{Adv}^{\pm}}(f).

Moreover, the catalyst of the transducer
S_{f}
is as in (
8.7
).
In particular, it executes the input oracle only on its admissible subspace.

9
Composition of Transducers

In this section, we describe basic properties of transducers.
In particular, we show how to combine simple transducers in order to obtain more complex ones. This is akin to quantum algorithms being build out of elementary operations and subroutines.
In this section, we mostly focus on the circuit model of computation.

9.1
Basic Properties

From Theorem
5.1
, it follows that, for a fixed
S
and
O
, the mappings
\xi\mapsto v(S,O,\xi)
and
\xi\mapsto q(S,O,\xi)
are linear.
In particular, for
c\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

W(S,O,c\xi)=|c|^{2}W(S,O,\xi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad L^{(i)}(S,O,c\xi)=|c|^{2}L^{(i)}(S,O,\xi).

(9.1)

Notice, however, that it is
not
necessarily true that
W(S,O,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})=W(S,O,\xi_{1})+W(S,O,\xi_{2})
even for orthogonal
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
.
On the other hand, it
is
the case that for
\xi_{1}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}
and
\xi_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}
, we have

W(S,O,\xi_{1}\oplus\xi_{2})=W(S,O,\xi_{1})+W(S,O,\xi_{2}),

using the extended definition of (
3.6
).

Proposition 9.1

(Inverse)
.

For a canonical transducer
S
, the inverse transducer
S^{-1}
satisfies
\tau\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S^{-1}(O^{*})}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\xi
whenever
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
.
Moreover,
S^{-1}
can be implemented in the canonical form, it has the same time complexity as
S
,

W(S^{-1},O^{*},\tau)=W(S,O,\xi),\qquad\text{and}\qquad q(S^{-1},O^{*},\tau)=(I\otimes O)q(S,O,\xi).

Proof.

Let
v=v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}
be the catalyst of the transduction
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
.
From (
5.1
), it is clear that if
S(O)
maps
\xi\oplus v\mapsto\tau\oplus v
, then
S(O)^{*}
maps
\tau\oplus v\mapsto\xi\oplus v
, hence, transduces
\tau
into
\xi
with the same catalyst.
One problem is that its action, as the inverse of (
7.2
),

\tau\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(S^{\circ})^{*}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}^{*}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}.

is not in the canonical form.
But we can take the following transducer
S^{-1}
in its stead:

\tau\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}^{*}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\tau\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(S^{\circ})^{*}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}.

It is in the canonical form, and satisfies all the conditions.
∎

9.2
Alignment

In the remaining part of this section, we will study different ways of combining transducers
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
.
First, we consider parallel composition of transducers
\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}
, where individual
S_{i}
act on orthogonal parts of the workspace.
Then we move onto sequential composition
S_{m}*S_{m-1}*\cdots*S_{1}
, where they act on the same space one after another.
Finally, we consider functional composition of two transducers, where the second transducer acts as an oracle for the first one.

We generally assume that all
S_{i}
use the same oracle
O
.
This is without loss of generality since if they use different sets of input oracles, we can assume they all use the oracle
O
which is the direct sum of the union of these sets of oracles.
Individual
S_{i}
will then just ignore the input oracles they are not using.

In principle, the spaces
\mathcal{H}_{i}
and
\mathcal{L}_{i}
can differ between different
S_{i}
, but we assume they are all embedded into some larger register
\mathcal{H}
, which also serves as
\mathcal{L}
per our convention of Section
5.2
.
What is crucial, though, is that all
S_{i}
use the same privacy qubit
\mathcal{P}
, the same query register
\mathcal{R}
, and, most importantly, the oracle
O
is applied to the same subset of registers in all
S_{i}
.
This is summarised by the following definition,
cf.
Remark
4.1
:

Definition 9.2

(Alignment)
.

We say that canonical transducers
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
are
aligned in the oracle
O^{(i)}
if the query of
O^{(i)}
is conditioned on the same value
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
of the same register and acts on the same subset of registers in all of them.
We say that
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
are
aligned
if they are aligned in all their oracles, and, additionally, use the same privacy qubit
\mathcal{P}
.

If the alignment condition is not satisfied, the transducers have to explicitly move their registers around to meet it.
This might take time if the registers are lengthy.

9.3
Parallel Composition

Parallel composition of two or several quantum programs is their execution as a direct sum on orthogonal parts of the space of the algorithm.
For transducers, parallel composition can be implemented in a straightforward way.

Definition 9.3

(Direct Sum of Transducers)
.

Let
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
be canonical transducers.
We assume they all use the same space
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
, the same input oracle
O\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
, and are aligned.
In particular, the query
\widetilde{O}=I_{\mathcal{H}}\oplus I^{\circ}\oplus I\otimes O
is given by (
7.1
) and is controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
in all of them.

Let the register
\mathcal{J}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
.
The direct sum
\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}
is a canonical transducer in the space
\mathcal{J}\otimes(\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L})=(\mathcal{J}\otimes\mathcal{H})\oplus(\mathcal{J}\otimes\mathcal{L})
.
It has the same input oracle
O
as all
S_{i}
.
The query is again controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.
The work unitary of
\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}
is
\bigoplus_{i}S^{\circ}_{i}
, where
S^{\circ}_{i}
is the work unitary of
S_{i}
.

Proposition 9.4

(Parallel Composition)
.

The canonical transducer
S=\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}
from Definition
9.3
satisfies the following conditions.
Assume
\xi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{i}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau_{i}
for all
i
.
Then,
S(O)
transduces
\xi=\bigoplus_{i}\xi_{i}
into
\tau=\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}
.
Moreover,

W(S,O,\xi)=\sum_{i}W(S_{i},O,\xi_{i})\qquad\text{and}\qquad q(S,O,\xi)=\bigoplus_{i}q(S_{i},O,\xi_{i}).

(9.2)

The time complexity of
S
is equal to the time complexity of implementing
\bigoplus_{i}S^{\circ}_{i}
.

Proof.

Recall that all
S_{i}
are aligned, and, hence, use the same space,
\mathcal{P}
is their common privacy qubit, and
\mathcal{R}
their common query register.
The privacy and the query registers of
S
will still be
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{R}
.

Let
v_{i}
be the catalyst of transduction
\xi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{i}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau_{i}
.
The initial coupling of
S
is

\xi\oplus v=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\bigr].

(9.3)

As required by canonicity, we first apply the input oracle
O
controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.
This has the effect that
O
is applied to all
S_{i}
in parallel.
Then, conditioned on the value
i
in
\mathcal{J}
, we apply the work unitary
S^{\circ}_{i}
to the last two registers.
By the assumption
\xi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{i}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau_{i}
, this gives

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\bigr]=\tau\oplus v.

Eq. (
9.2
) follows from (
9.3
).
∎

Remark 9.5

(Different input oracles in
S_{i}
)
.

In Definition
9.3
, we assume all
S_{i}
use the same input oracle
O
.
It is, however, possible to allow each
S_{i}
to use its own input oracle
O_{i}
so that the total input oracle of
\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}
is
\bigoplus_{i}O_{i}
, and
\xi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{i}(O_{i})}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau_{i}
.
This is used, for instance, in iterated and composed functions.
We account for this possibility by allowing
\mathcal{M}
to contain
\mathcal{J}
, so that the input oracle of
S_{i}
has read-only access to the value of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{E}}
.
This does not interfere with the proof of Proposition
9.4
, and we get the following identities instead of (
9.2
):

W(S,O,\xi)=\sum_{i}W(S_{i},O_{i},\xi_{i})\qquad\text{and}\qquad q(S,O,\xi)=\bigoplus_{i}q(S_{i},O_{i},\xi_{i}).

(9.4)

The time complexity of implementing
\bigoplus_{i}S_{i}
greatly depends on the structure of
S_{i}
and the computational model.
For instance, if we assume the QRAG model, implementation of
\bigoplus S_{i}
can be done in time essentially
\max_{i}T(S_{i})
as per Corollary
4.4
.
The important special case when all
S_{i}
are the same can be efficiently handled in the circuit model as well.
We write it out explicitly for ease of referencing.

Definition 9.6

(Multiplication by Identity)
.

Let
S
be a canonical transducer with space
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
, input oracle
O\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
, and query given by (
7.1
).
Let also
\mathcal{E}
be a space, and
I_{\mathcal{E}}
be the identity on
\mathcal{E}
.
We define
I_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes S
as a canonical transducer in the space
(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{H})\oplus(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{L})
, with the work unitary
I_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes S^{\circ}
.
In other words,
I_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes S=\bigoplus_{i}S
where the summation is over the basis of
\mathcal{E}
.

Corollary 9.7
.

In the settings of Definition
9.6
, assume
\xi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau_{i}
as
i
ranges over the basis of
\mathcal{E}
.
Then,
I_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes S(O)
transduces
\xi=\bigoplus_{i}\xi_{i}
into
\tau=\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}
.
The complexities are given by (
9.2
) or (
9.4
) with all
S_{i}=S
.
The time complexity is
T(I_{\mathcal{E}}\otimes S)=T(S)
.

Note that complexities in (
3.6
) are the same as in this corollary with Eq. (
9.2
) used.

9.4
Sequential Composition

A quantum program (
4.11
) is a sequence of gates applied one after the other.
Therefore, sequential composition is of prime importance in quantum algorithms.
Let us formally define it for transducers.

Definition 9.8

(Sequential Composition)
.

Let
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
be an aligned family of canonical transducers, all on the same public space
\mathcal{H}
and the same input oracle
O
.
Its sequential composition is a canonical transducer
S=S_{m}*S_{m-1}*\cdots*S_{1}
on the same public space with the following property.
For every sequence of transductions

\xi=\psi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{1}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\psi_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{2}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\psi_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{3}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{m-1}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\psi_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{m}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\psi_{m+1}=\tau,

(9.5)

S(O)
transduces the initial state
\xi
into the final state
\tau
.

The above definition does not specify the implementation as we give two different implementations in this section.
The first one is tailored towards the circuit model, and the second one towards the QRAG model.
This division is not strict though.

Proposition 9.9

(Sequential Composition, Sequential Implementation)
.

A sequential composition
S=S_{m}*S_{m-1}*\cdots*S_{1}
as in Definition
9.8
can be implemented by a canonical transducer with the following parameters:

W(S,O,\xi)=\sum_{t=1}^{m}W(S_{t},O,\psi_{t}),\qquad q(S,O,\xi)=\bigoplus_{t=1}^{m}q(S_{t},O,\psi_{t}),

(9.6)

and

T(S)=\mathcal{O}(m)+\sum_{t=1}^{m}T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{t}),

(9.7)

where
T_{\mathrm{C}}
is defined in Section
4.4
.

Proof.

The general idea is simple.
We first apply the input oracle to make the queries in all
S_{t}
in parallel.
After the query, we execute all
S^{\circ}_{t}
one after the other, see Figure
9.4
.
Some care must be taken, however, so that transducers act inside their respective private spaces and do not interfere with private spaces of other transducers.

S^{\circ}_{1}
v^{\circ}_{1}
v^{\circ}_{1}
v^{\bullet}_{1}
Ov^{\bullet}_{1}
v^{\bullet}_{1}
\psi_{2}
S^{\circ}_{2}
v^{\circ}_{2}
v^{\circ}_{2}
v^{\bullet}_{2}
Ov^{\bullet}_{2}
v^{\bullet}_{2}
\psi_{3}
S^{\circ}_{3}
v^{\circ}_{3}
v^{\circ}_{3}
v^{\bullet}_{3}
Ov^{\bullet}_{3}
v^{\bullet}_{3}
\psi_{4}
S^{\circ}_{4}
v^{\circ}_{4}
v^{\circ}_{4}
v^{\bullet}_{4}
Ov^{\bullet}_{4}
v^{\bullet}_{4}
I_{m}\otimes I\otimes O
\xi
\psi_{1}
\psi_{5}
\tau

The transducer
S
uses the same privacy and query registers
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{R}
as the family
S_{1},\dots,S_{m}
.
We additionally use an
m
-qudit
\mathcal{K}
, so that the local space of the transducer
S_{t}
is marked by the value
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.
Therefore, the space of
S
is of the form
\mathcal{K}\otimes(\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L})
.
Its public space is still
\mathcal{H}
embedded as
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
.

Let
\xi
and
\psi_{t}
be as in (
9.5
).
For each
t
, let
v_{t}
be the catalyst of the transduction
\psi_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{t}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\psi_{t+1}
from (
9.5
).
For the transduction
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
, we have the following initial coupling:

\xi\oplus v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}.

(9.8)

This already implies (
9.6
).

As required by the definition, the first operation is the application of the input oracle conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.
This gives the state

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\bigl|\widetilde{O}v_{t}\bigr>_{\mathcal{L}},

where
\widetilde{O}v_{t}
is defined as in (
7.7
), and we used that
\xi=\psi_{1}
.

The work part of the transducer
S
is as follows.
We assume the indexing of
\mathcal{K}
is done modulo
m
.

•

For
t=1,\dots,m
:

1.

Controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}
, replace the value
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

2.

Apply the work unitary
S^{\circ}_{t}
controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}
.

By induction and using (
7.6
), after
\ell
iterations of the loop, we have the state

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ell}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{\ell+1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{t=1}^{\ell}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{t=\ell+1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\bigl|\widetilde{O}v_{t}\bigr>_{\mathcal{L}}.

And after execution of the whole transducer
S
, we have the state

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}=\tau\oplus v,

where we used that
\psi_{m+1}=\tau
.
The time complexity in (
9.7
) can be achieved using the direct-sum finite automaton of Lemma
4.6
.
∎

The second implementation of the sequential composition is slightly less intuitive.
We move all the intermediate states in (
9.5
) to the catalyst.
This increases the catalyst size, but now all the operations
S^{\circ}_{t}
can be implemented in parallel.

Proposition 9.10

(Sequential Composition, Parallel Implementation)
.

A sequential composition
S=S_{m}*S_{m-1}*\cdots*S_{1}
as in Definition
9.8
can be implemented by a transducer with the following parameters:

W(S,O,\xi)=\sum_{t=2}^{m}\|\psi_{t}\|^{2}+\sum_{t=1}^{m}W(S_{t},O,\psi_{t}),\qquad q(S,O,\xi)=\bigoplus_{t=1}^{m}q(S_{t},O,\psi_{t})

(9.9)

and

T(S)=\mathcal{O}(\log m)+T\biggl(\bigoplus_{t}S^{\circ}_{t}\biggr).

(9.10)

In the QRAG model, we usually replace
\mathcal{O}(\log m)
in (
9.10
) by
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.

Proof.

The main new idea compared to Proposition
9.9
is that we do not compute the intermediate
\psi_{t}
from (
9.5
), but store the sequence
\psi_{2},\dots,\psi_{m}
in the catalyst.
We apply all of
S^{\circ}_{t}
in parallel, thus moving this sequence forward by one position,
see Figure
9.4
.

S^{\circ}_{1}
v^{\circ}_{1}
v^{\circ}_{1}
v^{\bullet}_{1}
Ov^{\bullet}_{1}
v^{\bullet}_{1}
\psi_{2}
\psi_{2}
S^{\circ}_{2}
v^{\circ}_{2}
v^{\circ}_{2}
v^{\bullet}_{2}
Ov^{\bullet}_{2}
v^{\bullet}_{2}
\psi_{3}
\psi_{3}
S^{\circ}_{3}
v^{\circ}_{3}
v^{\circ}_{3}
v^{\bullet}_{3}
Ov^{\bullet}_{3}
v^{\bullet}_{3}
\psi_{4}
\psi_{4}
S^{\circ}_{4}
v^{\circ}_{4}
v^{\circ}_{4}
v^{\bullet}_{4}
Ov^{\bullet}_{4}
v^{\bullet}_{4}
I_{m}\otimes I\otimes O
\xi
\psi_{1}
\psi_{5}
\tau

Again, we assume all
S
have space
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
, have privacy qubit
\mathcal{P}
, and query register
\mathcal{R}
.
The transducer
S
uses the same query register
\mathcal{R}
, but we introduce a new privacy qubit
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
We also use an
m
-qudit
\mathcal{K}
, so that the space of
S
is of the form
\mathcal{K}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\otimes(\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L})
.

Let
\xi
and
\psi_{t}
be as in (
9.5
).
For each
t
, let
v_{t}
be the catalyst of transduction
\psi_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{t}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\psi_{t+1}
.
We start with the following initial coupling

\xi\oplus v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\biggl[\sum_{t=2}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\biggr].

This already gives (
9.9
).
As required by the canonicity assumption, we first apply the input oracle.
This gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\biggl[\sum_{t=2}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widetilde{O}v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\biggr],

where
\widetilde{O}
is defined in (
7.7
).
Here we use that
\mathcal{H}
has value
0
in the register
\mathcal{R}
and is not affected by the input oracle.
Next, we exchange
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
.
Since
\xi=\psi_{1}
, this gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\biggl[\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widetilde{O}v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\biggr].

Now we apply
S^{\circ}_{t}
to the last two registers, controlled by the value in the register
\mathcal{K}
.
In other words, we apply
\bigoplus_{t}S^{\circ}_{t}
.
By (
7.6
), this gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\biggl[\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t+1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\biggr].

(9.11)

Now we increment the value in the register
\mathcal{K}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}
, and exchange
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|m+1}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
.
Since
\tau=\psi_{m+1}
, we get

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P^{\prime}}}\biggl[\sum_{t=2}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\biggr]=\tau\oplus v.

(9.12)

The time complexity estimate is obvious.
∎

9.5
Functional Composition

We defined functional composition in Section
2.1
as an algorithm where a subroutine is used to implement an oracle call.
In the case of transducers, this falls under the transitivity of transduction, Proposition
5.4
, as part of the transducer (the oracle call) is implemented as a transduction action of another transducer.
In this section, we give an explicit construction for canonical transducers.

We assume the settings similar to Figure
2.3
,
except we use transducers
S_{A}
and
S_{B}
instead of programs
A
and
B
.
The outer transducer
S_{A}
has two input oracles
O\oplus O^{\prime}
joined as in Section
7.2
.
The first one,
O
, is the global input oracle, and the second one,
O^{\prime}
, is the oracle realised as the transduction action of the inner transducer
S_{B}
.
We assume both transducers are in the canonical form and aligned in the oracle
O
.
We denote their public spaces by
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
, respectively.
If there are several subroutines implemented by different transducers, then, as in Figure
2.3
, they can be joined via parallel composition of Proposition
9.4
.

Proposition 9.11

(Functional Composition)
.

Under the above assumptions, there exists a canonical transducer
S_{A}\circ S_{B}
with the public space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and the oracle
O
, which satisfies the following properties.

•

Its transduction action on input oracle
O
is equal to the transduction action of
S_{A}
on oracle
O\oplus O^{\prime}
, where
O^{\prime}=S_{B}(O)\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}_{B}}
.

•

Its transduction complexity satisfies

W(S_{A}\circ S_{B},O,\xi)=W(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)+W\Bigl(S_{B},O,q^{(1)}(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)\Bigr).

(9.13)

•

Its total query state is

q(S_{A}\circ S_{B},O,\xi)=q^{(0)}(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)\oplus q\Bigl(S_{B},O,q^{(1)}(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)\Bigr).

(9.14)

•

Its time complexity is
T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{A})+T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{B})
.

Here
q^{(0)}
and
q^{(1)}
denote the partial query states of
S_{A}
to the oracles
O
and
O^{\prime}
, respectively.
We also used the extended versions of
W
and
q
from (
3.6
) for the transducer
S_{B}
.

Note that both Equations (
9.13
) and (
9.14
) are reminiscent of the “gold standard” (
2.4
).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the space of the input oracle
O
.
The space of the transducer
S_{A}
is of the form

\mathcal{H}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{A}
, where the last two spaces are for the queries to
O
and
O^{\prime}
, respectively.
In particular,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{A}=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
by the assumption that
S_{B}(O)
implements
O^{\prime}
.

Let
v=v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\oplus v^{\prime}
be the catalyst for the transduction
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
by
S_{A}(O\oplus O^{\prime})
.
That is,
v^{\bullet}=q^{(0)}(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)
and
v^{\prime}=q^{(1)}(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)
.
By (
7.6
), the transducer
S_{A}
imposes the following chain of transformations in
\mathcal{H}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{A}
:

\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\oplus v^{\prime}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\oplus v^{\prime}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O^{\prime}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\oplus(I\otimes O^{\prime})v^{\prime}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S^{\circ}_{A}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\tau\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\oplus v^{\prime},

(9.15)

where we separated the applications of
O
and
O^{\prime}
.
Recall that the identity
I
acts on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}_{A}
.

The space of
S_{B}
is
\mathcal{H}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{B}
, where
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{B}=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{M}
.
We obtain the transducer
I\otimes S_{B}
as in Definition
9.6
, whose space is
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}_{A}\otimes(\mathcal{H}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{B})
.

Let
w=w^{\circ}\oplus w^{\bullet}
be the catalyst for the transduction
v^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}(I\otimes O^{\prime})v^{\prime}
by
I\otimes S_{B}(O)
. In particular,
w^{\bullet}=q\bigl(S_{B},O,q^{(1)}(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)\bigr)
.
By (
7.6
) again, we have the chain of transformations in
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}_{A}\otimes(\mathcal{H}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{B})
:

v^{\prime}\oplus w^{\circ}\oplus w^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;v^{\prime}\oplus w^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes I_{B}\otimes O)w^{\bullet}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I\otimes S^{\circ}_{B}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;(I\otimes O^{\prime})v^{\prime}\oplus w^{\circ}\oplus w^{\bullet},

(9.16)

where
I_{B}
is the identity on
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}_{B}
.

\xi
v^{\circ}
v^{\circ}
v^{\prime}
w^{\circ}
w^{\bullet}
(I\oplus I\otimes I_{B})\otimes O
Ow^{\bullet}
I\otimes S^{\circ}_{B}
Ov^{\bullet}
O^{\prime}v^{\prime}
w^{\circ}
w^{\bullet}
S^{\circ}_{A}
\xi
v^{\circ}
v^{\bullet}
v^{\prime}

The transducer
S_{A}\circ S_{B}
works as follows; see Figure
9.5
. It starts in

\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\oplus v^{\prime}\oplus w^{\circ}\oplus w^{\bullet}.

(9.17)

It applies the input oracle
O
to the third and the last terms, which corresponds to the first steps in both (
9.15
) and (
9.16
).
This gives

\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\oplus v^{\prime}\oplus w^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes I_{B}\otimes O)w^{\bullet}.

(9.18)

Then it performs
I\otimes S^{\circ}_{B}
, which is the second operation in (
9.16
), and which gives

\xi\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus(I\otimes O)v^{\bullet}\oplus(I\otimes O^{\prime})v^{\prime}\oplus w^{\circ}\oplus w^{\bullet}.

(9.19)

After that,
S^{\circ}_{A}
is performed, which is the last operation of (
9.15
), and we get the final state

\tau\oplus v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}\oplus v^{\prime}\oplus w^{\circ}\oplus w^{\bullet}.

(9.20)

The transduction (
9.13
) and the query (
9.14
) complexities follow from (
9.17
).
In order to get the time complexity, we have to elaborate on the placement of all these transducers in registers.
Since
S_{A}
and
S_{B}
are aligned in
O
, we assume they use the same value
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
of the same register to denote its execution.
Concerning
O^{\prime}
, which is an oracle only for
S_{A}
, we assume it is indicated by another qubit
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}_{B}
be the privacy qubits of
S_{A}
and
S_{B}
, respectively; we assume they are different.

Let us write down the values of these indicator qubits for the various subspaces of the composed transducer:

\begin{array}[]{l|cccccc|}&\mathcal{H}_{A}&\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{A}&\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{A}&\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{A}\text{ and }\mathcal{H}_{B}&\mathcal{L}^{\circ}_{B}&\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}_{B}\\
\hline\cr\mathcal{P}_{A}&0&1&1&1&1&1\\
\mathcal{R}&0&0&1&0&0&1\\
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}&0&0&0&1&1&1\\
\mathcal{P}_{B}&0&0&0&0&1&1\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Other than that, we assume we can embed all these subspaces into registers also preserving
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{A}=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
The privacy qubit of
S_{A}\circ S_{B}
is
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and its query register is
\mathcal{R}
.

Let us go through the steps of
S_{A}\circ S_{B}
.
The application of the query to
O
to get to (
9.18
) is conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
as required, and it is possible because we assume
S_{A}
and
S_{B}
are aligned in
O
.
The application of
I\otimes S^{\circ}_{B}
to get to (
9.19
) is conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R^{\prime}}}
.
Finally, the application of
S^{\circ}_{A}
to get to the final state (
9.20
) is conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P_{B}}}
.
This gives the required time complexity estimate.
∎

10
Transducers from Programs

In this section, we describe how to convert a quantum program
A
like in (
4.11
) into a canonical transducer
S_{A}
, and give the corresponding corollaries.
We consider both the circuit and the QRAG models.
The general idea is similar in both cases.
First, we obtain transducers for individual gates, and then compose them using either Proposition
9.9
for the circuit model, or Proposition
9.10
for the QRAG model.

10.1
General Assumptions

We are given a program
A
, and our goal is to construct a canonical transducer
S_{A}
whose transduction action is identical to the action of
A
.

One thing to observe is that we have to modify our assumptions on the execution of input oracles by introducing an additional register related to
\mathcal{R}
.
There are two main reasons for that.
First, the initial state
\xi
of
S_{A}
can be any state in
\mathcal{H}
.
In particular, it can use
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
which is in contradiction with the assumption of Section
7.1
that
\xi
must be contained in
\mathcal{H}^{\circ}
.
Second, as described in Section
3.4
, the catalyst in the QRAG case is the history state (
3.11
).
Various
\psi_{t}
can use
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
, and we would like to protect them from the application of the input oracle in
S_{A}
.

Because of that, we introduce an additional qubit
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
.
The input oracle in the canonical transducer
S_{A}
is applied conditioned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}
.
More precisely, the
i
-th input oracle is controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Additionally, we assume that all the queries made by the program
A
are aligned, see Definition
9.2
, and, in case of several programs, they are aligned by their shared input oracles.
This is necessary since the composition results of Section
9
require the transducers to be aligned.

10.2
Building Blocks

Here we describe transducers corresponding to elementary operations.
The following proposition is trivial.

Proposition 10.1

(Trivial Transducer)
.

Let
A
be a quantum algorithm (without oracle calls) that implements some unitary in
\mathcal{H}
.
It can be considered as a transducer with
\mathcal{L}
being empty,
T(A)
being the time complexity of
A
, and both
W(A,\xi)
and
q(A,\xi)
equal to zero.

Oracle execution is more tricky because of the canonicity assumption.
Recall the query
\widetilde{O}=I^{\circ}\oplus I\otimes O
from (
4.1
) taking place in
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
with
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{H}^{\uparrow}\otimes\mathcal{M}
.
Let us divide
\xi=\xi^{\circ}\oplus\xi^{\bullet}
accordingly.

Proposition 10.2

(Oracle)
.

For a fixed embedding
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\uparrow}\otimes\mathcal{M}
as above, there exists a canonical transducer
S_{\mathrm{Q}}
such that
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{\mathrm{Q}}(O)}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\widetilde{O}\xi
for all
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
and unitaries
O\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
.
The transducer satisfies
W(S_{\mathrm{Q}},O,\xi)=\|\xi^{\bullet}\|^{2}
,
q(S_{\mathrm{Q}},O,\xi)=\xi^{\bullet}
, and
T(S_{\mathrm{Q}})=O(1)
.

Proof.

The private space of
S_{\mathrm{Q}}
will be
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}\otimes\mathcal{M}
with
\mathcal{L}^{\uparrow}
equal to
\mathcal{H}^{\uparrow}
.
The catalyst is
v=v^{\bullet}=\xi^{\bullet}
.
The transducer
S_{\mathrm{Q}}
first applies the input oracle to
\mathcal{L}^{\bullet}
, which is achieved by conditioning on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}
:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\bigl|(I\otimes O)\xi^{\bullet}\bigr>_{\mathcal{L}}\\
=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(I\otimes O)\xi^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

Now apply C-NOT to both
\mathcal{P}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
.
This gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(I\otimes O)\xi^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\bigl|\widetilde{O}\xi\bigr>_{\mathcal{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\bigl|\xi^{\bullet}\bigr>_{\mathcal{L}}.\qed

10.3
Circuit Model

For the circuit model, we have the following result, which is a first half of Theorem
3.5
.

Theorem 10.3

(Program to Transducer, Circuit Model)
.

Let
A=A(O)
be a quantum program in some space
\mathcal{H}
assuming the circuit model.
We assume all the queries in
A
are aligned, see Definition
9.2
.
Then, there exists a canonical transducer
S_{A}(O)
with the following properties.

•

S_{A}(O)\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}}=A(O)
for all input oracles
O
.

•

For any input oracle
O
and the initial state
\xi
, we have
q(S_{A},O,\xi)=q(A,O,\xi)
. In particular,
L(S_{A},O,\xi)=L(A,O,\xi)
.

•

The catalyst
v(S_{A},O,\xi)
is equal to the query state
q(S_{A},O,\xi)
. In particular,
W(S_{A},O,\xi)=L(A,O,\xi)
.

•

Finally, the transducer
S_{A}
can be implemented in the circuit model, and
T(S_{A})=T_{\mathrm{C}}(A)+\mathcal{O}(Q(A))=\mathcal{O}(T(A))
, where the complexity measures of
A
are as in Section
4.4
.

Proof.

Take the representation of the query algorithm as in (
4.2
).
We interpret each unitary
U_{t}
as a transducer using Proposition
10.1
, and transform each query into an independent copy of the transducer
S_{\mathrm{Q}}
from Proposition
10.2
.

Now, we apply sequential composition of Proposition
9.9
with
m=2Q(A)+1
.
The total query state
q(A,O,\xi)
is defined as the direct sum of all the queries given to the input oracle, hence, the third and the second points follow from (
9.6
).
The time complexity follows from (
9.7
) using that
T(A)=Q(A)+\sum_{t}T(U_{t})
.
∎

While canonical transducers are nice from the theoretical point of view, designing one from scratch is usually inconvenient.
The following result shows that we can convert any transducer into a canonical form with a slight increase in complexity.

Let
S=S(O)
be a transducer in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
not in the canonical form.
Recall from Section
5.1
that we defined its total query state
q_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi)
, and Las Vegas query complexity
L_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi)
on
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
as that of
S
, considered as a usual quantum algorithm in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
, on the initial state
\xi\oplus v(S(O),\xi)
, where
v\bigl(S(O),\xi\bigr)
is as in Theorem
5.1
.
We assume all the queries made in
S
are aligned.

Proposition 10.4

(Transforming Transducers into Canonical Form)
.

For a non-canonical transducer
S=S(O)
as above, there exists a canonical transducer
S^{\prime}=S^{\prime}(O)
with the same transduction action and such that, for all
O
and
\xi
,

q(S^{\prime},O,\xi)=q_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi),\qquad W(S^{\prime},O,\xi)=W(S(O),\xi)+L_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi),

(10.1)

and
T(S^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}(T(S))
.

Proof.

Let
S_{S}=S_{S}(O)
be a canonical transducer as obtained in Theorem
10.3
from
S=S(O)
considered as a quantum program.
Its public space is
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
, and
S_{S}(O)\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}}=S(O)
.

Let
\xi^{\prime}=\xi\oplus v\bigl(S(O),\xi\bigr)
. By Theorem
10.3
, we have
T(S_{S})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(S)\bigr)
, and

v_{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}}(S_{S},O,\xi^{\prime})=q_{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}}(S_{S},O,\xi^{\prime})=q_{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}}(S,O,\xi^{\prime})=q_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi),

(10.2)

where, in the third expression, we consider
S
as a usual quantum program in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
.

We obtain
S^{\prime}=S^{\prime}(O)
as the transduction action of
S_{S}(O)
on
\mathcal{H}
, using the transitivity of transduction, Proposition
5.4
.
First,
S_{S}(O)
is in canonical form when considered as a transducer with the public space
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
.
A fortiori, it is canonical also on the public space
\mathcal{H}
.
Next, its time complexity does not change, hence,
T(S^{\prime})=T(S_{S})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(S)\bigr)
.
For the query state, from (
5.3
) and (
10.2
), we get

q_{\mathcal{H}}(S^{\prime},O,\xi)=q_{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}}(S_{S},O,\xi^{\prime})=q_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi).

Finally, for the transduction complexity, we can utilise (
5.3
) in the following way:

\displaystyle W(S^{\prime},O,\xi)=W_{\mathcal{H}}\bigl(S_{S}(O),\xi\bigr)

\displaystyle=W_{\mathcal{H}}\bigl(S_{S}(O)\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}},\xi\bigr)+W_{\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}}\bigl(S_{S}(O),\xi^{\prime}\bigr)

\displaystyle=W_{\mathcal{H}}(S(O),\xi)+L_{\mathcal{H}}(S,O,\xi),

where we used (
10.2
) in the last equality.
∎

The remaining corollaries of Theorem
10.3
were already proven in Section
3
: Theorems
3.6
and
3.8
.

Let us note that Theorem
10.3
might not always be the best way to get a transducer
S_{A}
from a quantum program
A
in the circuit model.
One can use additional structure of
A
to get better transducers.
For instance, if
A
repeatedly uses the same sequence of gates, one can define it as a new oracle, thus reducing the time complexity of the transducer, see, e.g., Proposition
11.3
.
If
A
contains a large loop, one can use Proposition
9.10
.
Obtaining efficient transducers in the circuit model for specific cases seems like an interesting research direction.

10.4
QRAG Model

Assuming the QRAG model, we get the remaining half of Theorem
3.5
.

Theorem 10.5
.

Let
A=A(O)
be a quantum program in some space
\mathcal{H}
, and assume we have QRAM access to the description of
A
.
Then, there exists a canonical transducer
S_{A}(O)
with the following properties.

•

S_{A}(O)\scalebox{0.7}{\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathcal{H}}=A(O)
for all input oracles
O
.

•

For any input oracle
O
and initial state
\xi
, we have
q(S_{A},O,\xi)=q(A,O,\xi)
. In particular,
L(S_{A},O,\xi)=L(A,O,\xi)
.

•

Also,
W(S_{A},O,\xi)\leq T(A)\|\xi\|^{2}
.

•

Finally, in the QRAG model, we have
T(S_{A})=\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
as defined in (
3.13
).

Proof.

The proof parallels that of Theorem
10.3
using Proposition
9.10
instead of Proposition
9.9
, but since this special case might be of interest, we give an explicit construction here, slightly simplifying it along the way.

It is convenient to assume a different but essentially equivalent form of a quantum algorithm.
Namely, we assume that the algorithm
A
is given as

A(O)=G_{m-1}\widetilde{O}^{b_{m-1}}G_{m-2}\cdots\widetilde{O}^{b_{2}}G_{1}\widetilde{O}^{b_{1}}G_{0},

(10.3)

where each
G_{i}
is a gate (not a query),
\widetilde{O}
is the query operator (
4.1
), and each
b_{t}
is a bit that indicates whether there is a query before the
t
-th gate (
b_{0}
is always 0).
A program like in (
4.11
) can be transformed into (
10.3
) with
m\leq T+1
adding identity
G_{t}
if necessary.
We assume we have QRAM access to an array specifying the gates
G_{t}
as well as to the array of
b_{t}
.

Let the algorithm
A
go through the following sequence of states on the initial state
\xi
and the oracle
O
:

\xi=\psi_{0}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G_{0}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\psi_{1}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G_{1}\widetilde{O}^{b_{1}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\psi_{2}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G_{2}\widetilde{O}^{b_{2}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\cdots\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G_{m-1}\widetilde{O}^{b_{m-1}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\psi_{m}=\tau.

(10.4)

At high level, the action of
S_{A}
is

\xi\oplus v=\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{O}^{b_{t}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigl|\widetilde{O}^{b_{t}}\psi_{t}\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{G_{t}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t+1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\longmapsto\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}=\tau\oplus v,

(10.5)

where, we first apply the input oracle conditioned on
b_{t}
, then
G_{t}
conditioned on
t
using Theorem
4.3
, and then increment
t
by 1 modulo
m
.

Recall that we have a decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\circ}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}
of the space of
A
, which is indicated by the query register
\mathcal{R}
.
As mentioned in Section
10.1
, the transducer
S_{A}
uses a different query qubit
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
.
We will use notation
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
.
Let
D
denote the C-NOT on
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
controlled by
\mathcal{R}
.
Then, for
\psi=\psi^{\circ}\oplus\psi^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|D\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi^{\circ}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi^{\bullet}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an
m
-qudit with operations modulo
m
, and
\mathcal{P}
be the privacy qubit of
S_{A}
.

Let us go through the steps of (
10.5
).
The initial coupling is given by

\xi\oplus v=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}+\sum_{t=1}^{m-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl|D^{b_{t}}\psi_{t}\bigr>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}.

First, as required by the canonical form and our assumptions in Section
10.1
, we apply the input oracle
O
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}}
.
This acts as
\widetilde{O}
on
D\psi_{t}
, but does not change
\psi_{t}
.
Then, we apply the operation
D
controlled on
b_{t}
(which can be accessed using the QRAM).
After that, we apply C-NOT to
\mathcal{P}
controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}
.
This gives the second state in the sequence (
10.5
):

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl|\widetilde{O}^{b_{t}}\psi_{t}\bigr>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}.

Now, we apply the last two operations from (
10.5
):
G_{t}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}
, and increment of
\mathcal{T}
by 1 modulo
m
.
This gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{t=1}^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{t}}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}.

Now we apply the operation
D
controlled on
b_{t}
and apply C-NOT to the register
\mathcal{P}
controlled by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|m}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}
.
This gives the final state

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{m}}}\right>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}+\sum_{t=1}^{m-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl|D^{b_{t}}\psi_{t}\bigr>_{\mathcal{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}}=\tau\oplus v.

Since
\|\psi_{t}\|=\|\xi\|
, we have that

W(S_{A},O,\xi)=(m-1)\|\xi\|^{2}\leq T(A)\|\xi\|^{2}.

It is clear that
q(S_{A},O,\xi)=q(A,O,\xi)
, and the time complexity of
S_{A}
is
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
thanks in particular to Theorem
4.3
.
∎

In applications like in Section
3.5
, we often have to apply this construction in parallel.
The following easy modification of the proof takes care of that.

Proposition 10.6
.

Let
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
be quantum programs in some space
\mathcal{H}
, all using the same oracle
O
.
Assume we have QRAM access to their joint description.
Then, the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}S_{A_{i}}
of transducers defined in Theorem
10.5
can be implemented in time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.

By the joint access, we mean that we have access to the list
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}
of the parameters
m
in (
10.3
), as well as access to
b_{t}
and
G_{t}
of
A_{i}
as a double array with indices
i
and
t
.

Proof of Proposition
10.6
.

We execute the transducers
S_{A_{i}}
in parallel using a register
\mathcal{J}
to store the value of
i
.
Accessing
b_{t}
and
G_{t}
now is double-indexed by
i
and
t
, and by our general assumption of (
3.13
) it takes time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
to access them.
For incrementation of
\mathcal{T}
modulo
m
, we use the array containing
m_{i}
.
Other than that, it is a standard word-sided operation and takes time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.
∎

The consequences of this result were already considered in Section
3
: Theorems
3.6
,
3.10
, and
3.11
.

11
Example III: Iterated Functions

In this section, we give a more detailed proof of Theorem
3.12
.
We will use notation of Section
8.2
for the transducer
S_{f}
built from the adversary bound.
However, we assume a more general variant of a canonical transducer
S_{f}
for evaluation of
f\colon[q]^{n}\to[q]
.
Its public space is
\mathbb{C}^{q}
, its admissible space is spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{f}(\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}})}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|f(x)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
for all
x\in[q]^{n}
.
We assume the initial coupling of
S_{f}
on
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
is given by

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\oplus v_{x}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v^{\circ}_{x}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\uparrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl|v^{\uparrow}_{x,i}\bigr>_{\mathcal{W}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\downarrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\bigl|v^{\downarrow}_{x,i}\bigr>_{\mathcal{W}}\Bigr]

(11.1)

for some vectors
v^{\circ}_{x}
,
v^{\uparrow}_{x,i}
, and
v^{\downarrow}_{x,i}
, where the registers are as in (
8.7
).
The difference between (
11.1
) and (
8.7
), however, is addition of the term
v^{\circ}_{x}
that is not processed by the input oracle.
Note that (
11.1
) gives the general form of a canonical transducer that executes the input oracle on the admissible subspace: the constituent
O_{x,i}
from (
8.5
) on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
and
O_{x,i}^{*}
on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.

Recall the definition of the composed function
f\circ g
from (
3.29
):

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}f\circ g\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}

\displaystyle(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m},\;\;z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m},\;\;\dots\dots,\;\;z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})

(11.2)

\displaystyle=f\bigl(g(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m}),g(z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m}),\dots,g(z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})\bigr).

We define

\vec{y}_{i}=(z_{i,1},\dots,z_{i,m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad x=\bigl(g(\vec{y}_{1}),g(\vec{y}_{2}),\dots g(\vec{y}_{n})\bigr)

(11.3)

so that
f(x)=\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}f\circ g\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}(z)
.
Recall also notation
W_{x}(S)=W\bigl(S,\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)
and
W(S)=\max_{x}W_{x}(S)
from (
8.2
).

Proposition 11.1
.

Let
S_{f}
and
S_{g}
be canonical transducers for the functions
f
and
g
of the form described above.
Then, there exists a canonical transducer
S_{f\circ g}
for the composed function
f\circ g
from (
11.2
) that has the same form, and such that

W_{z}(S_{f\circ g})=W_{x}(S_{f})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f})\cdot W_{\vec{y}_{i}}(S_{g})\leq W(S_{f})+L(S_{f})\cdot W(S_{g})

(11.4)

and

L_{z}(S_{f\circ g})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f})\cdot L_{\vec{y}_{i}}(S_{g})\leq L(S_{f})\cdot L(S_{g}),

(11.5)

for every
z\in[q]^{nm}
.
Time complexity satisfies
T(S_{f\circ g})=T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{f})+2T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{g})
.

Proof.

As the first step, we create a bidirectional version
\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}}
of
S_{g}
.
Its public space is
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{Q}
, and its admissible space on the input oracle
\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}}
is spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\uparrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\downarrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|g(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Its corresponding transduction action is

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\uparrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\uparrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|g(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\downarrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|g(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\downarrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}.

Its time complexity is
2T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{g})
and, in notation at the end of Section
3.3
:

W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}}\bigr)=W_{y}(S_{g})\qquad\text{and}\qquad L_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}}\bigr)=L_{y}(S_{g}).

We obtain
\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}}
as follows.
By swapping
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
if necessary, we may identify
\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}}^{*}
and
\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}}
.
Then from Proposition
9.1
, we obtain a transducer
S^{-1}_{g}
with transduction action
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|g(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
on
\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}}
, whose complexity is identical to
S_{g}
.
We may assume
S_{g}
and
S^{-1}_{g}
are aligned.
We get
\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}}
as
S_{g}\oplus S_{g}^{-1}
, where the direct sum is done via the register
\mathcal{B}
.
By the definition of
W_{\mathrm{max}}
, we have that for some unit vector
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
:

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{max}}(\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}})

\displaystyle=W\Bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}},\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\uparrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\downarrow}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|g(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{Q}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=|\alpha|^{2}W\bigl(S_{g},\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)+|\beta|^{2}W\bigl(S_{g}^{-1},\overleftrightarrow{O_{y}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|g(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)=W_{y}(S_{g}),

where we used Propositions
9.4
and
9.1
, as well as (
9.1
).
Query complexity derivation is similar.

As the next step, we construct the transducer
I_{n}\otimes\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}}
, where
I_{n}
acts on the span of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{R}}
with
i>0
.
Moreover, as in Remark
9.5
, we assume the input oracle uses the register
\mathcal{R}
.
Therefore, the transduction action of
I_{n}\otimes\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}}
on the input oracle

\overleftrightarrow{O_{z}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\overleftrightarrow{O_{\vec{y}_{i}}}

is identical to the action of
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
