on its admissible subspace.

Finally, we get
S_{f\circ g}
as
S_{f}\circ\bigl(I_{n}\otimes\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}}\bigr)
.
The time complexity estimate follows from Proposition
9.11
.
For the transduction complexity, we obtain from (
3.21
), using that
S_{f}
makes only admissible queries to
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
and does not have direct access to
\overleftrightarrow{O_{z}}
:

\displaystyle W_{z}(S_{f\circ g})

\displaystyle=W\bigl(S_{f\circ g},\overleftrightarrow{O_{z}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq W\bigl(S_{f},\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\vec{y}_{i}}}\bigr)L^{(i)}(S_{f},\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})

\displaystyle=W_{x}(S_{f})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{\vec{y}_{i}}(S_{g})\cdot L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f}).

The last inequality in (
11.4
) follows from
L_{x}(S_{f})=\sum_{i}L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f})
.
The estimate (
11.5
) is similar, where we again use that
S_{f}
does not make direct queries to
\overleftrightarrow{O_{z}}
.
Finally, since
\overleftrightarrow{S_{g}}
makes only admissible queries to
O_{y}
, we get that
S_{f\circ g}
makes only admissible queries to
O_{z}
.
∎

Now we can prove a more detailed version of Theorem
3.12
.

Theorem 11.2
.

Let
S_{f}
be a canonical transducer for a function
f
of the form as in (
11.1
), and such that
L=L(S_{f})=1+\Omega(1)
. Let
W=W(S_{f})
and
T=T(S_{f})
, assuming the circuit model.
Then, for each
d
, there exists a bounded-error quantum algorithm evaluating the iterated function
f^{(d)}
in Monte Carlo query complexity
\mathcal{O}(L^{d})
and time complexity
\mathcal{O}\bigl(d\cdot TWL^{d-1}\bigr)=\mathcal{O}_{f}(d\cdot L^{d})
in the circuit model.

Proof.

Define
S_{f^{(d)}}
as
S_{f}
composed with itself
d
times using Proposition
11.1
.
By induction, we have that
L\bigl(S_{f^{(d)}}\bigr)\leq L^{d}
, and

W\bigl(S_{f^{(d)}}\bigr)\leq\bigl(1+L+\cdots+L^{d-1}\bigr)W=\mathcal{O}\bigl(L^{d-1}W\bigr).

Similarly, its time complexity is at most
2d\cdot T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{f})=\mathcal{O}(d\cdot T)
.
The theorem follows from Theorem
7.1
.
∎

The time complexity of the previous theorem can be slightly improved.
Let
s
denote the initial space complexity of the transducer
S_{f}
, i.e, the number of qubits used to encode the initial coupling
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\oplus v_{x}
.
It can be much smaller than the time complexity of
S_{f}
, as well as its space complexity, which is the total number of qubits used by
S_{f}
.

Proposition 11.3
.

The time complexity of Theorem
11.2
can be improved to
\mathcal{O}\bigl((sd+T)WL^{d-1}\bigr)
, where
s
is the initial space complexity of the transducer
S_{f}
.

We only sketch the proof, as it does not improve the overall asymptotic
\mathcal{O}_{f}(d\cdot L^{d})
.

Proof sketch of Proposition
11.3
.

By studying the proof of Theorem
11.2
, we can observe that
the work unitary of
S_{f^{(d)}}
consists of repeated applications of
\overleftrightarrow{S_{f}}
to different registers.
We can treat
\overleftrightarrow{S_{f}}
as an oracle.
Then, the work unitary of
S_{f^{(d)}}
becomes a non-canonical transducer with the oracle
\overleftrightarrow{S_{f}}
.
It is non-aligned, but we can make it aligned in additional time
s\cdot d
by switching the registers before each execution of
\overleftrightarrow{S_{f}}
.
We can convert it into the canonical form using Proposition
10.4
, which
increases the transduction complexity by at most a constant factor.
However, now it takes only one execution of
\overleftrightarrow{S_{f}}
and
\mathcal{O}(sd)
other operations to implement the transducer.
The statement again follows from Theorem
7.1
.
∎

12
Perturbed Transducers

It is quite common in quantum algorithm to use subroutines that impose some error.
The total correctness of the algorithm then follows from a variant of Lemma
4.5
, assuming that the error of each constituent is small enough.

In most cases in this paper, like in Sections
6
and
8.2
, the transduction action is the exact implementation of the required transformation.
However, it is not always feasible, and it makes sense to study what happens if a transducer satisfies the condition (
5.1
) only approximately.

It is worth noting that small errors in a transducer can result in large errors in the corresponding transduction action.
In other words, it is possible that
\xi\oplus v\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\tau^{\prime}\oplus v^{\prime}
with
v
close to
v^{\prime}
, but
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
with
\tau
being far from
\tau^{\prime}
.
For example, let both
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{L}
be 1-dimensional, and assume
S
acts as

S\colon\begin{pmatrix}0\\
v\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a\\
\sqrt{v^{2}-a^{2}}\end{pmatrix}

for some positive real
v
and
a
.
Observe that

v-\sqrt{v^{2}-a^{2}}=\frac{a^{2}}{v+\sqrt{v^{2}-a^{2}}}

can be very small for large
v
, even if
a
is substantial.
Thus, on the basis of
v\approx v-\sqrt{v^{2}-a^{2}}
we may be tempted to assume that
S
approximately transduces
0
into
a
, but this is very far from the true transduction action
0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}0
.

12.1
Definition

In order to solve this issue, we incorporate a perturbation, in the sense of Lemma
4.5
, into the transducer
S
.
Let
S
be a unitary in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
that maps

\widetilde{S}\colon\xi\oplus v\mapsto\widetilde{\tau}\oplus\widetilde{v}.

We assume its idealised version maps

S\colon\xi\oplus v\mapsto\tau\oplus v

with the perturbation

\delta(S,\xi)=\bigl\lVert(\tau\oplus v)-(\widetilde{\tau}\oplus\widetilde{v})\bigr\rVert.

We call
S
the idealised or perturbed transducer, and
\widetilde{S}
the approximate transducer.
We say that
S
transduces
\xi
into
\tau
, and we keep notation
\xi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\tau
,
v(S,\xi)=v
,
W(S,\xi)=\|v\|^{2}
, and
\tau(S,\xi)=\tau
.

This also extends to the canonical form of transducer in Section
7.1
, where we decompose
v=v^{\circ}\oplus v^{\bullet}
, and let
q(S,O,\xi)=v^{\bullet}
and
L(S,O,\xi)=\|v^{\bullet}\|^{2}
.
As before, we write
\delta(S,O,\xi)
instead of
\delta(S(O),\xi)
, and similarly for other pieces of notation.

As for usual quantum algorithms, we can use approximate transducers instead of the idealised ones, as long as we carefully keep track of the perturbations.
In the remaining part of this section, we will briefly study the main results of this paper under the perturbation lenses.

12.2
Implementation

We have the following variant of Theorem
5.5
.

Theorem 12.1
.

Under the assumptions of Section
12.1
, for every positive integer
K
, there exists a quantum algorithm that transforms
\xi
into
\tau^{\prime}
such that

\bigl\lVert\tau^{\prime}-\tau(S,\xi)\bigr\rVert\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{W(S,\xi)}{K}}+\sqrt{K}\delta(S,\xi)

for every
\widetilde{S}\colon\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{L}
, perturbed version
S
, and initial state
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
.
The algorithm conditionally executes
\widetilde{S}
as a black box
K
times, and uses
\mathcal{O}(K)
other elementary operations.

Proof.

The algorithm is identical to that of Theorem
5.5
.
The analysis is similar with the only difference that, on Step 2(a), in order to obtain the mapping

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\xi}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}\;\longmapsto\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau}}\right>_{\mathcal{H}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|t}}\right>_{\mathcal{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{P}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|v}}\right>_{\mathcal{L}}

as in (
5.7
), we introduce a perturbation of size
\delta(S,\xi)/\sqrt{K}
.
By Lemma
4.5
, the total perturbation is then

2\frac{\|v\|}{\sqrt{K}}+K\cdot\frac{\delta(S,\xi)}{\sqrt{K}},

which gives the required estimate.
∎

Again, this theorem can be reformulated as follows.

Corollary 12.2
.

For all
W,\varepsilon>0
, there exists a quantum algorithm that executes
\widetilde{S}
as a black box
K=\mathcal{O}(1+W/\varepsilon^{2})
times, uses
\mathcal{O}(K)
other elementary operations, and
\varepsilon
-approximately transforms
\xi
into
\tau(S,\xi)
for all
S
,
\widetilde{S}
, and the initial state
\xi
that satisfy
W(S,\xi)\leq W
and
\delta(S,\xi)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{K}}
.

We get a version of Theorem
7.1
in a similar fashion.

Proposition 12.3
.

Theorem
7.1
works assuming a perturbed transducer
S
. The estimate is

\bigl\lVert\tau^{\prime}-\tau(S,O,\xi)\bigr\rVert\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{K}}\sqrt{W(S,O,\xi)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{K}{K^{(i)}}-1}}\right)L^{(i)}(S,O,\xi)}+\sqrt{K}\delta(S,O,\xi).

Corollary
7.2
also works assuming a perturbed transducer
S
under the additional assumption of
\delta(S,O,\xi)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{K}}
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to Theorem
12.1
: we use perturbation of size

\delta(S,O,\xi)/\sqrt{K}
to get from (
7.14
) to (
7.15
).
∎

12.3
Composition

The composition of perturbed transducers exactly follows the corresponding constructions of Section
9
, where we use Lemma
4.5
to evaluate the total perturbation.
This gives us the following proposition.

Proposition 12.4
.

Propositions
9.4
,
9.9
,
9.10
and
9.11
work assuming perturbed transducers.
We get the following estimates:

\delta(S,O,c\xi)=|c|\delta(S,O,\xi),

(12.1)

\delta(S,O,\xi)\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta(S_{i},O,\xi_{i})^{2}}

(12.2)

for Proposition
9.4
, and

\delta(S,O,\xi)\leq\sum_{t=1}^{m}\delta(S_{t},O,\psi_{t})

(12.3)

for Propositions
9.9
and
9.10
.
For Proposition
9.11
, we have

\delta(S_{A}\circ S_{B},O,\xi)\leq\delta\bigl(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi\bigr)+\delta\bigl(S_{B},O,q^{(1)}(S_{A},O\oplus O^{\prime},\xi)\bigr).

(12.4)

Proof.

Eq. (
12.1
) follows by linearity.
All the remaining estimates follow from the corresponding proofs in Section
9
replacing each transducer with its approximate version and using Lemma
4.5
.
The estimate (
12.2
) follows from the observation that perturbations in terms of the direct sum act on orthogonal subspaces.
∎

13
Purifiers

In this section, we formulate and prove the formal version of Theorem
3.14
, as well as draw some consequences of it for composition of bounded-error algorithms.

13.1
Boolean Case

Recall our settings from Section
3.6
.
The input oracle performs the transformation

O_{\psi}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}

(13.1)

for some unit vector
\psi
in some space
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{N}
with
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and it is promised that there exist constants
0\leq c-d<c+d\leq 1
such that

either
\|\psi_{1}\|^{2}\leq c-d

or
\|\psi_{1}\|^{2}\geq c+d
,

(13.2)

which corresponds to
f(\psi)=0
and
f(\psi)=1
, respectively.
Let

\mu=\sqrt{(1-c)^{2}-d^{2}}+\sqrt{c^{2}-d^{2}}<1.

(13.3)

We say the input oracle
O_{\psi}
is
admissible
if it satisfies (
13.1
) and (
13.2
).

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
D
be a positive integer.
In the above assumptions, there exists a perturbed transducer
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
on the 1-dimensional public space and with bidirectional access to
O_{\psi}
which satisfies the following conditions:

•

It transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
into
(-1)^{f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
for all admissible input oracles
O_{\psi}
.
In particular, every vector in its public space is admissible.

•

On every admissible input oracle and normalised input state, its perturbation is at most
\delta_{\mathrm{pur}}=2\mu^{D-1}
.

•

Its transduction and query complexities,
W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})
and
L_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})
, are
\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/(1-\mu)\bigr)=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

•

It executes the input oracle on the admissible subspace only:
O_{\psi}
on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}
and
O_{\psi}^{*}
on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}
.

In the circuit model, the purifier can be implemented in time
\mathcal{O}(D\cdot s)
, where
s
is the number of qubits used in
\mathcal{M}
.
In the QRAG model and assuming the RA input oracle, the purifier can be implemented in time
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.

Here we used
W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})
to denote maximal
W(S_{\mathrm{pur}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})
over all admissible input oracles
O_{\psi}
.

L_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})
is defined similarly.

Proof.

The outline of the proof was already given in Section
3.6
.
We will assume that
D
is even for concreteness, the case of odd
D
being analogous.
Recall the parameters

a=\sqrt[4]{\frac{1-c+d}{1-c-d}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad b=\sqrt[4]{\frac{c+d}{c-d}}.

and the following vector in
\mathcal{M}
:

\widetilde{\psi}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{a}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},&\text{if $f(\psi)=0$};\\
a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}+\frac{1}{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}},&\text{if $f(\psi)=1$}.\\
\end{cases}

We have the following important estimate. If
f(\psi)=0
:

\|\widetilde{\psi}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{a^{2}}\|\psi_{0}\|^{2}+b^{2}\|\psi_{1}\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{a^{2}}(1-c+d)+b^{2}(c-d)=\mu,

(13.4)

where we used that
1/a^{2}<1<b^{2}
.
Similarly, for
f(\psi)=1
:

\|\widetilde{\psi}\|^{2}={a^{2}}\|\psi_{0}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{b^{2}}\|\psi_{1}\|^{2}\leq{a^{2}}(1-c-d)+\frac{1}{b^{2}}(c+d)=\mu.

(13.5)

We describe the transducer as non-canonical, and we will turn it into the canonical form later.
The space of the transducer is
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes D-1}
, where
\mathcal{D}
is a
D
-qudit.
The one-dimensional public space is spanned by
\xi=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-1}
.
The initial coupling is given by

\xi\oplus v=\sum_{i=0}^{D-1}(-1)^{i\cdot f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-i-1}.

(13.6)

The transduction complexity is

\|v\|^{2}\leq\|\xi\oplus v\|^{2}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\bigl\lVert\widetilde{\psi}\bigr\rVert^{2i}=\frac{1}{1-\bigl\lVert\widetilde{\psi}\bigr\rVert^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{1-\mu}.

(13.7)

0
1
2
3
D-1
D
\scriptstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-1}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle(-1)^{f(\psi)}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-2}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes 2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-3}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|2}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}
\cdots
\scriptstyle(-1)^{f(\psi)}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes D-1}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|D-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}

Let us now describe the action of the transducer.
The transducer is a multidimensional quantum walk on the line graph, see Figure
13.1
.
It is a product of two reflections
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.
The reflection
R_{1}
is the product of the local reflections on the odd vertices,
i=1,3,5,\dots,D-1
.
The reflection
R_{2}
is the product of the local reflections on the even vertices,
i=2,4,\dots,D-2
.

The local reflection for the vertex
i=1,\dots,D-1
is as follows.
It acts in
\spn\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes D-1}
.
Define a qubit
\mathcal{A}
whose value
0
corresponds to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}
and
1
to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}
.
If
i
is odd, this could be the least significant qubit of
\mathcal{D}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}=\mathcal{B}^{(i)}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{(i)}
be the
i
-th multiplier in the tensor product
\mathcal{M}^{\otimes D-1}
.
The reflection is as follows:

1.

Execute the input oracle
O_{\psi}
on
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}
.

2.

Execute a two-qubit unitary on
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{(i)}
, which is the reflection about the span of the states

\bigl(a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}+b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}\bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}.

3.

Execute the inverse oracle
O^{*}_{\psi}
on
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}
conditioned on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Claim 13.2
.

The local reflection for the vertex
i
multiplies the corresponding part of the state in (
13.6
)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-i}+(-1)^{f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-i-1}

by the phase
(-1)^{f(\psi)}
.

Proof.

After application of the oracle in Step 1, we get the state

\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\Bigl[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}+(-1)^{f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl|\widetilde{\psi}\bigr>_{\mathcal{M}}\Bigr]\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}^{\otimes D-i-1}.

The local reflection acts on the state in the square brackets, which can be rewritten as

\Bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}+\frac{1}{a}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}+\Bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}+b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}

(13.8)

if
f(\psi)=0
, and

\Bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}-a\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{0}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}+\Bigl(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}-\frac{1}{b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{A}}\Bigr)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|1}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{1}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}

(13.9)

if
f(\psi)=1
.
It is easy to see that the operation on Step 2 does not change the state in (
13.8
) and negates the one in (
13.9
), from which the claim follows.
∎

From the claim, it immediately follows that, if
f(\psi)=0
, the transducer does not change the state (
13.6
).
Therefore, in this case
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S_{\mathrm{pur}}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.

On the other hand, if
f(\psi)=1
, then
R_{1}
reflects the whole state
\xi\oplus v
, and
R_{2}
reflects all the terms in the sum except for
i=0
and
i=D-1
. Thus, the final state is

-\xi\oplus v-2(-1)^{(D-1)f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|D-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\lvert\widetilde{\psi}\rangle^{\otimes D-1}.

This can be interpreted as transducing
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
into
-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
with a perturbation of size at most
2\mu^{D-1}
by (
13.4
) and (
13.5
).

One can see that the local reflection for the vertex
i
applies the input oracle and its inverse on the part of the state
v
in the subspace
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i-1}}\right>_{\mathcal{D}}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes D-1}
.
Hence, the query complexity is at most
2\|\xi\oplus v\|^{2}\leq 2/(1-\mu)
by (
13.7
).
The transformation into canonical form, Proposition
10.4
, adds the query complexity to the transduction complexity, hence, the latter stays
\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/(1-\mu)\bigr)
.

Let us estimate time complexity. We start with the circuit model.
The queries in transducer of Theorem
13.1
are not aligned, as they are applied to different copies of
\mathcal{M}
.
In order to make them aligned, as required by Proposition
10.4
, the register
\mathcal{M}^{(i)}
should be moved to some specific array of qubits shared by all the local reflections.
This takes time
\mathcal{O}(s)
per each local reflection.
Step 2 of the local reflection can be implemented in constant time.
Thus, each local reflection takes time
\mathcal{O}(s)
.
There are
D-1
local reflections performed.
By Lemma
4.6
with all
\phi_{i}
being absent, the whole transducer can be implemented in time
\mathcal{O}(D\cdot s)
.
Transformation into canonical form in Proposition
10.4
keeps the time complexity of the transducer essentially the same.

Now consider the QRAG model.
All local reflections in
R_{1}
can be performed in parallel, and the same is true for
R_{2}
.
The first and the third operation in the local reflection are implemented by the RA input oracle.
The second operation can be performed in
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
time by Theorem
4.3
.
∎

13.2
Non-Boolean Case

This time let
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{N}
be a space with
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{C}^{p}
.
Let
O_{\psi}
be an oracle that performs the following state generation:

O_{\psi}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{j}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}.

(13.10)

Let
d>0
be a constant.
We assume that for every
\psi
there exists (unique)
f(\psi)\in[p]
such that

\|\psi_{f(\psi)}\|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}+d.

(13.11)

Define

\mu=2\sqrt{1/4-d^{2}}<1,

which is the same as in (
13.3
) for
c=1/2
.
We treat
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{C}^{p}
as composed out of
\ell=\log p
qubits.
We do
not
assume that
\ell=\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
here, as functions, in principle, can have very long output.
Again, we call every input oracle
O_{\psi}
satisfying (
13.10
) and (
13.11
) admissible.

Theorem 13.3
.

Let
D
be a positive integer.
Under the above assumptions, there exists a perturbed transducer
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
on the public space
\mathbb{C}^{p}
and with bidirectional access to
O_{\psi}
which satisfies the following conditions:

•

For all
b\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, and admissible
O_{\psi}
, it transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
into
\bigl|b\oplus f(\psi)\bigr>_{\mathcal{}}
, where
\oplus
stands for the bit-wise XOR.
In particular, every initial vector in
\mathbb{C}^{p}
is admissible.

•

On any admissible input oracle and unit initial vector, its perturbation is at most
\delta_{\mathrm{pur}}=2\mu^{D-1}
.

•

Its query complexity satisfies
L_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/(1-\mu)\bigr)=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

•

It executes the input oracle on the admissible subspace only:
O_{\psi}
on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}
and
O_{\psi}^{*}
on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}
.

For the time and the transduction complexity, we have the following estimates:

•

In the circuit model,
W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/(1-\mu)\bigr)=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
T(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=\mathcal{O}(s\cdot D)
, where
s
is the number of qubits used in
\mathcal{M}
.

•

In the QRAG model, assuming the RA input oracle, we have
W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=\mathcal{O}(\log p/(1-\mu))=\mathcal{O}(\log p)
and
T(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.

Here we use
W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})
to denote maximal
W(S_{\mathrm{pur}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},\xi)
over all admissible input oracles
O_{\psi}
and admissible normalised initial states
\xi
.

L_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})
is defined similarly.

Proof.

We reduce the non-Boolean case to the Boolean case of Theorem
13.1
by encoding the value into the phase and using the Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm
[
18
]
to decode it back.

For simplicity of notation, we will assume
p=2^{\ell}
so that
O_{\psi}
in (
13.10
) just does not use the extra dimensions.
For
a,b\in[p]
, we denote by
a\odot b\in\{0,1\}
their inner product when considered as elements of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\ell}
.

Denote the public space
\mathbb{C}^{p}
of
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
by
\mathcal{J}
.
We first apply the Hadamard
H^{\otimes\ell}
to perform the following transformation

H^{\otimes\ell}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\longmapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{i\odot b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}.

(13.12)

Consider the following procedure
E
that evaluates the inner product between
i
and the output of the oracle into an additional qubit
\mathcal{Z}
:

E(O_{\psi})\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Z}}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle O_{\psi}}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{j}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Z}}\longmapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{j}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i\odot j}}\right>_{\mathcal{Z}}.

We consider it as a direct sum
E=\bigoplus_{i\in[p]}E^{(i)}
with

E^{(i)}(O_{\psi})\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Z}}\longmapsto\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right>_{\mathcal{B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi_{j}}}\right>_{\mathcal{N}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i\odot j}}\right>_{\mathcal{Z}}.

(13.13)

We convert them into canonical transducers
S_{E}
and
S^{(i)}_{E}
.
We have
L_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(S_{E}^{(i)}\bigr)=1
, where the admissible subspace is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{M}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{Z}}
.
Using Propositions
9.1
and
9.4
, we get transducers

\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}}\bigr)=S_{E}(O_{\psi})\oplus S_{E}^{-1}(O^{*}_{\psi})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}}\bigr)=S_{E}^{(i)}(O_{\psi})\oplus(S_{E}^{(i)})^{-1}(O^{*}_{\psi}).

Again,

L_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}\bigr)=1
.
We still have
\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}=\bigoplus_{i}\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}
with the help of Remark
9.5
.
It is also clear that
\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}
only executes the input oracle
\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}}
on the admissible subspace.

We treat
E^{(i)}(O_{\psi})
as an oracle encoding a Boolean value into the register
\mathcal{Z}
.
By (
13.11
), we get that
E^{(i)}(O_{\psi})
evaluates
i\odot f(\psi)
with bounded error.
Take the purifier
S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}
from Theorem
13.1
with
c=1/2
and the same values of
d
and
D
.
This purifier satisfies

S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\Bigl(\overleftrightarrow{E^{(i)}(O_{\psi})}\Bigr)\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}(-1)^{i\odot f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}.

Taking direct sum over all
i\in[p]
, we get that a transducer

\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}I_{\mathcal{J}}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}\Bigl(\overleftrightarrow{E(O_{\psi})}\Bigr)=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{p-1}S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\Bigl(\overleftrightarrow{E^{(i)}(O_{\psi})}\Bigr)

(13.14)

performs the following transduction:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{i\odot b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{i\odot b+i\odot f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}.

(13.15)

Finally, we again apply
H^{\otimes\ell}
to get

H^{\otimes\ell}\colon\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{i\odot b+i\odot f(\psi)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}\longmapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus f(\psi)}}\right>_{\mathcal{J}}.

(13.16)

Combining (
13.12
), (
13.15
) and (
13.16
), we see that we can use sequential composition of Proposition
9.9
to get

S_{\mathrm{pur}}=S_{H^{\otimes\ell}}*\bigl((I_{\mathcal{J}}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}\bigr)*S_{H^{\otimes\ell}},

where
S_{H^{\otimes\ell}}
is a transducer from Proposition
10.1
with transduction action
H^{\otimes\ell}
and no input oracle.

Let us estimate query complexity on a unit vector
\xi\in\mathcal{J}
.
We have the following estimate, where we explain individual lines after the equation.

\displaystyle L(S_{\mathrm{pur}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},\xi)

\displaystyle=L\bigl((I_{\mathcal{J}}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},H^{\otimes\ell}\xi\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}L\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},\phi_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}L_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\bigr)L_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}\bigr)\|\phi_{i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(1/(1-\mu))\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}\|\phi_{i}\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1/(1-\mu)).

On the first line, we used sequential composition of Proposition
9.9
and that the transducer
S_{H^{\otimes\ell}}
does not use the input oracle.
On the second line, we decomposed
H^{\otimes\ell}\xi=\bigoplus_{i}\phi_{i}
, and used (
13.14
) and Proposition
9.4
.
On the third line, we used functional composition of Proposition
9.11
, Eq. (
9.1
), and that
S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}
does not have direct access to
O_{\psi}
and executes its input oracle
E^{(i)}
on the admissible subspace only.

In a similar way, we have

\displaystyle W(S_{\mathrm{pur}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},\xi)

\displaystyle=2W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})+W\bigl((I_{\mathcal{J}}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},H^{\otimes\ell}\xi\bigr)

\displaystyle=2W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})+\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}W\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},\phi_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})+\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}\Bigl[W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\bigr)+L_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime}\bigr)W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}\bigr)\Bigr]\|\phi_{i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})+W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})+L_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})\max_{i}W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})+\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/(1-\mu)\bigr)\max_{i}\Bigl(1+W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}\bigr)\Bigr).

(13.17)

For the perturbation, we have using Proposition
12.4
:

\displaystyle\delta(S_{\mathrm{pur}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},\xi)

\displaystyle=\delta\bigl((I_{\mathcal{J}}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}},\overleftrightarrow{O_{\psi}},H^{\otimes\ell}\xi\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}\delta\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime},E^{(i)}(O_{\psi}),\phi_{i}\bigr)^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}\bigl(\delta_{\mathrm{pur}}\|\phi_{i}\|\bigr)^{2}}=\delta_{\mathrm{pur}}.

Finally,

T(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=2T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})+T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})+T_{\mathrm{C}}(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}})+\mathcal{O}(1).

(13.18)

In the circuit model, the transduction complexities of
S_{H^{\otimes\ell}}
and
\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)}
are 0 and 1, respectively, and we get the required estimate from (
13.17
).
Also, both
T(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})
and
T(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}})
are
\mathcal{O}(\log p)
, and
T(S_{\mathrm{pur}}^{\prime})=\mathcal{O}(s\cdot D)
. Since
s\geq\log p
, we get the required estimate from (
13.18
).

In the QRAG model, we have both
W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{H^{\otimes\ell}})
and
W_{\mathrm{max}}(\overleftrightarrow{S_{E}}^{(i)})
bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\log p)
, which gives the required estimate on the transduction complexity.
For the time complexity, all the terms in (
13.18
) are
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
, which shows that
T(S_{\mathrm{pur}})=\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.
∎

13.3
Composition of Bounded-Error Algorithms

Purifier can be composed with algorithms evaluating functions with bounded error to reduce the error.
In this section, we mention some examples.

Since we are ignoring the constant factors, we will assume the standard version of the input oracle:
O_{x}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.
In particular, it is its own inverse, and we use
O_{x}
instead of
\overleftrightarrow{O_{x}}
everywhere in this section.

Let us again recall the composed function
f\circ g
from (
11.2
):

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}f\circ g\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}

\displaystyle(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m},\;\;z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m},\;\;\dots\dots,\;\;z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})

(13.19)

\displaystyle=f\bigl(g(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m}),g(z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m}),\dots,g(z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})\bigr).

and

\vec{y}_{i}=(z_{i,1},\dots,z_{i,m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad x=\bigl(g(\vec{y}_{1}),g(\vec{y}_{2}),\dots g(\vec{y}_{n})\bigr)

(13.20)

so that
f(x)=\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}f\circ g\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}(z)
.
The following result is essentially Theorem
3.15
.

Theorem 13.4
.

Let
A
and
B
be quantum algorithms in the circuit model that evaluate functions
f
and
g
, respectively, with bounded error.
Then, there exists an algorithm in the circuit model that evaluates the function
f\circ g
with bounded error in time complexity

\mathcal{O}(L)\bigl(T(A)+T(B)+s\log L\bigr)

(13.21)

where
L
is the worst-case Las Vegas query complexity of
A
, and
s
is the space complexity of
B
.
The algorithm makes
\mathcal{O}\bigl(L\cdot Q(B)\bigr)
queries, where
Q(B)
is the usual Monte Carlo query complexity of
B
.

Proof.

First, use Theorem
10.3
to get a transducer
S_{A}
whose transduction action is identical to the execution of
A
.
Its time complexity
T(S_{A})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(A)\bigr)
and its transduction and query complexities are bounded by
L
.

The algorithm
B
on the input oracle
O_{y}
evaluates
g(y)
with bounded error.
We obtain the algorithm
B^{-1}
with the input oracle
O_{y}^{*}=O_{y}
whose action is the inverse of
B
.
Combining the two via direct sum, we get the algorithm
\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{y})=B(O_{y})\oplus B^{-1}(O_{y})
.
Let
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
be the corresponding purifier from Theorem
13.3
, and
D
and
\delta_{\mathrm{pur}}
be the parameters therein.
The transduction action of
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
on the input oracle
\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{y})
is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus g(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.

By (
13.20
), we have
O_{z}=\bigoplus_{i}O_{\vec{y}_{i}}
.
Let us denote by
\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{z})
the algorithm
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}I_{n}\otimes\overleftrightarrow{B}\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}(O_{z})=\bigoplus_{i}\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{\vec{y}_{i}})
.
By Corollary
9.7
with Remark
9.5
, the transducer
I_{n}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}}
on the input oracle
\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{z})
performs the transduction
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus g(\vec{y}_{i})}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
for every
i\in[n]
.
In other words, its transduction action is
O_{x}
.

Now consider the transducer

S=S_{A}\circ(I_{n}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}})

with the input oracle
\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{z})
.
By the definition of functional composition, it transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
into
\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle($}}g\circ f\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle)$}}(z)
.
By (
3.21
), its transduction complexity is at most

W(S_{A},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})+\sum_{i}L^{(i)}(S_{A},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})\cdot W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}},\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{\vec{y}_{i}})\bigr)=\mathcal{O}(L).

We obtain the required algorithm by using Corollary
12.2
with
\varepsilon=\Theta(1)
on the above transducer
S
.
The transducer
S
is executed
\mathcal{O}(L)
times.
Each execution takes times
\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(A)+sD\bigr)
to execute the transducer and
\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(B)\bigr)
to execute the input oracle.
Therefore, the total time complexity is

\mathcal{O}(L)\bigl(T(A)+T(B)+sD\bigr)

and the query complexity is
\mathcal{O}(L)Q(B)
.

It remains to estimate
D
.
We may assume the error of
A
is a small enough constant.
By Corollary
12.2
, in order to get a bounded-error algorithm from the transducer
S_{A}\circ(I_{n}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}})
, we should have

\delta\bigl(I_{n}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}},\overleftrightarrow{B}(O_{z}),q(S_{A},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})\bigr)=\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/\sqrt{L}\bigr).

for a small enough constant.
Using (
12.1
) and that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert q(S_{A},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})}}\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{L}
, we get that it suffices to have
\delta_{\mathrm{pur}}=\mathcal{O}(1/L)
.
Therefore, we can take
D=\mathcal{O}(\log L)
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Let us now proceed with QRAG case, Theorem
3.16
.
Recall the function from (
3.40
):

f\bigl(g_{1}(z_{1,1},\dots,z_{1,m}),g_{2}(z_{2,1},\dots,z_{2,m}),\dots,g_{n}(z_{n,1},\dots,z_{n,m})\bigr).

(13.22)

with notation

\vec{y}_{i}=(z_{i,1},\dots,z_{i,m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad x=\bigl(g_{1}(\vec{y}_{1}),g_{2}(\vec{y}_{2}),\dots g_{n}(\vec{y}_{n})\bigr).

For simplicity, we assume all functions
f
and
g
use the same input and output alphabet
q
.
Let
A
and
B_{i}
be quantum algorithms that evaluate
f
and
g_{i}
, respectively.
To simplify expressions, we assume that
T(B_{i})\geq\log q
. In other words, we spend at least 1 iteration per bit of the output.
Also, all the algorithms have the same upper bound on permissible error.
We use an approach similar to Section
11
on iterated functions, so that we are able to compose several layer of functions.

As in Section
8.2
, we denote
L_{x}(A)=L(A,O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})
and similarly for other notation.
This time, however the input oracle
O_{x}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus x_{i}}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
is uniquely defined.
As we can make the perturbation of a purifier as small as necessary without increasing complexity, we ignore the perturbations in the following implicitly assuming they are small enough.

Theorem 13.5
.

Let
S_{f}
be a perturbed transducer evaluating the function
f
, and
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
be algorithms evaluating the functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
with bounded-error.
Assuming the QRAG model with RA input oracle, and QRAM access to the description of
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
, there exists a perturbed transducer
S
evaluating the function (
13.22
) with the following parameters.
Its transduction complexity is

W(S,O_{z},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=W(S_{f},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}\bigl(L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f})T(B_{i})\bigr)

(13.23)

its query complexity is

L^{(i,j)}(S,O_{z},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f})L^{(j)}_{\vec{y}_{i}}(B_{i})\bigr)

(13.24)

and its time complexity is
T(S)=T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{f})+\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.

Proof.

From Theorem
10.5
, for each
i
, we obtain a transducer
S_{B_{i}}
whose transduction action is identical to the action of
B_{i}
.
Its transduction complexity
W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{B_{i}})=\mathcal{O}\bigl(T(B_{i})\bigr)
and query state is identical to that of
B_{i}
.
Also, we obtain
\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}}=S_{B_{i}}\oplus S_{B_{i}}^{*}
whose complexity is identical to
S_{B_{i}}
.
Let
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
be the corresponding purifier.
We have that the transduction action of
S_{\mathrm{pur}}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}}
on an input oracle
O_{y}
is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus g_{i}(y)}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
.

Using direct sum, we get that the transducer

S_{g}=(I_{n}\otimes S_{\mathrm{pur}})\circ\bigoplus_{i}\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}}=\bigoplus_{i}S_{\mathrm{pur}}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}}

on the input oracle
O_{z}=\bigoplus_{i}O_{\vec{y}_{i}}
transduces
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|i}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b\oplus g_{i}(\vec{y}_{i})}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}
for every
i\in[n]
.
Thus, its transduction action is
O_{x}
,
and the transducer
S=S_{f}\circ S_{g}
evaluates the function in (
13.22
).

Let us estimate its transduction complexity.
First by (
3.17
):

W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}},O_{\vec{y}_{i}})\leq W_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})+L_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})W_{\mathrm{max}}(\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}})=\mathcal{O}(\log p)+\mathcal{O}(T(B_{i}))=\mathcal{O}(T(B_{i}))

using our assumption on
T(B_{i})\geq\log p
.
Therefore, by (
3.21
):

\displaystyle W\bigl(S,O_{z},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq W(S_{f},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})+\sum_{i}L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f})W_{\mathrm{max}}\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}},O_{\vec{y}_{i}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq W(S_{f},O_{x},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}})+\sum_{i}L^{(i)}_{x}(S_{f})\mathcal{O}(T(B_{i})).

For the query complexity, we use a partial-query variant of (
3.19
) and that the purifier only executes the subroutine on the admissible initial states to obtain:

L_{\mathrm{max}}^{(j)}\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}},O_{\vec{y}_{i}}\bigr)\leq L_{\mathrm{max}}(S_{\mathrm{pur}})L^{(j)}\bigl(S_{B_{i}},O_{\vec{y}_{i}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)=\mathcal{O}\bigl(L^{(j)}_{\vec{y}_{i}}(B_{i})\bigr).

Hence, by (
3.24
):

L^{(i,j)}\bigl(S,O_{z},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0}}\right>_{\mathcal{}}\bigr)\leq L^{(i)}_{x}(A)L_{\mathrm{max}}^{(j)}\bigl(S_{\mathrm{pur}}\circ\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}},O_{\vec{y}_{i}}\bigr)\leq L^{(i)}_{x}(A)\mathcal{O}\bigl(L^{(j)}_{\vec{y}_{i}}(B_{i})\bigr).

The time complexity of
S_{\mathrm{pur}}
is
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.
Also, the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i}\overleftrightarrow{S_{B_{i}}}
can be implemented in
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
by Proposition
10.6
.
Thus, the time complexity of
S
is
T_{\mathrm{C}}(S_{f})+\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathrm{R}})
.
∎

We get Theorem
3.16
from this theorem by using a transducer
S_{f}
obtained from the program
A
using Theorem
10.5
, and then applying Proposition
12.3
to the resulting transducer.
Moreover, we get that the algorithm executes the input oracle
O_{z}

\mathcal{O}\Bigl(\max_{z}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{(i)}_{x}(A)L_{\vec{y}_{i}}(B_{i})\Bigr)

times.

Theorem
13.5
can be used multiple times in a row to obtain a composed transducer for a tree of functions similar to the one in Theorem
3.11
.
If
d
is the depth of the tree, the query complexity grows by the factor of
C^{d}
, where
C
is the constant in (
13.24
).
This growth is completely analogous to what one obtains using span programs for the query complexity.
The contribution to the time complexity from the subroutines on layer
\ell
also grows by the factor of
C^{\ell}
because they are multiplied by the corresponding query complexity in (
13.23
).
The time complexity of the final transducer is
\mathcal{O}(dT_{\mathrm{R}})
.
